Multiple mixing and fractional cohomological equation Thanks: 1 Based on research supported by NSF grant DMS-1845416 Thanks: Key words and phrases: Fractional cohomological equation, representation theory, multiple exponential mixing
Zhenqi Jenny Wang 1

Abstract
. We introduce the notion of the (multiple) fractional cohomological equation and, by studying its solutions, develop a novel framework to obtain the decay of matrix coefficients for partially hyperbolic algebraic actions. In particular, we show that mere partial Hölder regularity of L 2 L^{2} vectors is sufficient for exponential decay of matrix coefficients. As an application, under the assumption of ergodicity, we obtain explicit and sharp exponential mixing rates of all orders for a large class of partially hyperbolic algebraic actions. Furthermore, we introduce the concept of irrational automorphisms on nilmanifolds and prove that these automorphisms exhibit super-exponential mixing of all orders, marking the first such example in the literature.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Abelian algebraic actions and mixing

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a
connected Lie group,
\Gamma
a (cocompact) torsion free lattice in

\mathbb{G}
, and
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{G}
a closed abelian subgroup which
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Let
L
be a compact subgroup of the centralizer
Z(\mathcal{Z})
of

\mathcal{Z}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}
acts by left translation on the manifolds

\mathcal{X}=L\backslash\mathbb{G}/\Gamma
, which is called
an algebraic
\mathcal{Z}
-action
and is denoted by
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
. We say that
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
is rank one if
m+l=1
.

The linear part
d\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
of
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
is induced by the adjoint representation of
\mathcal{Z}
on
\text{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
. Define the Lyapunov exponents of
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
as the log’s of the absolute values of the eigenvalues of
d\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
. We get linear functionals
\chi:\,\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
, which are called
Lyapunov functionals
of
d\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
.

There are three main classes of partially hyperbolic algebraic abelian
actions described by Katok and Spatzier in their seminal work
[
18
]
:

(1)

the symmetric space examples
: suppose
G
is a closed subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
and is a connected semisimple Lie group of the non-compact
type with finite center. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a closed abelian subgroup of
G
;

(2)

the twisted symmetric space examples
: suppose
G
is a closed subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
and is a connected semisimple Lie group of the non-compact
type with finite center and
V
is a simply connected nilpotent group of
\mathbb{G}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a closed abelian subgroup of
G\ltimes V
;

(3)

for suspensions of partially hyperbolic automorphisms of nilmanifolds take
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{R}^{m}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{R}^{m}\ltimes N
, where
N
is a simply connected nilpotent Lie
group.

\mathcal{X}
carries a unique
\mathbb{G}
-invariant
probability measure
\varrho
, locally given by the Haar measure of
\mathbb{G}
. Given
n\geq 2
, we say that
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}:\mathcal{Z}\to\text{Aut}(\mathcal{X})
is:

•

n
-mixing
if for every
f_{1}\cdots,f_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and every
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}
, we have

\int_{\mathcal{X}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\big(\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}(z_{i})x\big)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{i}(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)

as
\min_{i\neq j}\lVert z_{i}z_{j}^{-1}\rVert\to+\infty
.

•

n
-exponential-mixing with rate
\eta
if there exist
\eta,\,r,\,C>0
such that for every
f_{1}\cdots,f_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and every
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathcal{X}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\big(\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}(z_{i})x\big)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{i}(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\eta\min_{i\neq j}\lVert z_{i}z_{j}^{-1}\rVert}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{r}(f_{i})

where
\mathcal{S}_{r}(f_{i})
denotes the Sobolev
norm of order
r
introduced in Section 1 below.

•

n
-super-exponential-mixing
for any
\eta>0
there exist
r,\,C>0
such that for every
f_{1}\cdots,f_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and every
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathcal{X}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\big(\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}(z_{i})x\big)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{i}(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\eta\min_{i\neq j}\lVert z_{i}z_{j}^{-1}\rVert}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{r}(f_{i})

We say that mixing rate
\eta
is
effective
if it is explicitly determined by the Lyapunov exponents of
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
n
.

1.2.
Fractional derivative and cohomological equation

Suppose
S
is a Lie group. Let
(\pi,\mathcal{H})
be a unitary representation of
S
. Given
X\in\text{Lie}(S)
,
many problems in dynamics and ergodic theory can be reduced to the study of the cohomological equation

X\xi=\omega,

that is, the problem of finding a vector
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
whose Lie derivative
X\xi
equals a given vector
\omega\in\mathcal{H}
.

Next, we introduce the notion of
fractional derivative
. Let
S_{1}
be an abelian closed subgroup
S
which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Fix a basis
\mathfrak{u}=\{\mathfrak{u}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{u}_{m}\}
of
\text{Lie}(S_{1})
. For any
t=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, set

\displaystyle\exp(t)=\exp(t_{1}\mathfrak{u}_{1}+\cdots+t_{m}\mathfrak{u}_{m})\quad\text{ and }\quad\pi(t)=\pi(\exp(t)).

We consider the restricted representation
\pi\mid_{S_{1}}
.
By spectral theory, there exists a regular Borel measure
\mu
on
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
(called the associated measure of
\pi
with respect to
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
) such that every vector
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
can be decomposed as

\xi=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\xi_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi),

and for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\pi(\exp(t))\xi=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\chi(t)\,\xi_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi),

where
\chi(t)=e^{\mathrm{i}\chi\cdot t}
(here we identify
\mathbb{R}^{l}
with
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{l}}
).

For
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
1\leq i\leq m
we define the
fractional derivative
associated with
|\mathfrak{u}_{1}|^{r_{1}}|\mathfrak{u}_{2}|^{r_{2}}\cdots|\mathfrak{u}_{m}|^{r_{m}}
by:

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{u}_{1}|^{r_{1}}|\mathfrak{u}_{2}|^{r_{2}}\cdots|\mathfrak{u}_{m}|^{r_{m}}(\xi):=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}|\chi_{1}|^{r_{1}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{r_{m}}\xi_{\chi}d\mu(\chi).

Note
. Even when
r_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
1\leq i\leq m
, the above definition
is similar to, yet different from, the standard Lie derivative

\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{r_{1}}\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{r_{2}}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{m}^{r_{m}}(\xi):=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\mathrm{i}^{\,r_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}}\,\chi_{1}^{r_{1}}\chi_{2}^{r_{2}}\cdots\chi_{m}^{r_{m}}\,\xi_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi).

We will study the solution of the
fractional cohomological equation

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{u}_{1}|^{r_{1}}|\mathfrak{u}_{2}|^{r_{2}}\cdots|\mathfrak{u}_{m}|^{r_{m}}(\xi)=\omega

for a given vector
\omega\in\mathcal{H}
.

We will also study the solution of the
multiple fractional cohomological equation

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}|\mathfrak{u}_{i}|^{r_{i}}(\xi_{i})=\omega

again for a given vector
\omega\in\mathcal{H}
.

In the case where
S=\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\pi
is the regular representation on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
, the above definition of the fractional derivative, for sufficiently regular functions, is equivalent to those defined via the Fourier transform and, consequently, to the Riemann-Liouville, Caputo, and related approaches. To the best of the author’s knowledge, however, the formulation of fractional derivatives and the associated (multiple) fractional cohomological equations via spectral theory for groups beyond
\mathbb{R}^{m}
or
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
has not appeared previously in the literature.

1.3.
History of the study of mixing

Mixing is a key idea in understanding randomness in dynamical systems. It means that, two events become asymptotically independent as time tends to infinity–in other words, the system gradually “forgets” its initial state, leading to statistical independence over time. Higher order mixing also appears in many math problems, with useful applications in fields such as combinatorics (e.g., recurrence and partition regularity), arithmetic (e.g., equidistribution in number theory), and probability (e.g., validating limit theorems).

For partially hyperbolic actions on nilmanifolds
: It has been shown that mixing (without a specified rate) holds if for every nonzero
z\in\mathcal{Z}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{l}
,

\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
is ergodic, then
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
is
n
-mixing for all
n\geq 2
. In the case of tori, this result is due to Parry
[
24
]
(for
l=1
) and Schmidt and Ward
[
25
]
(for
l\geq 2
), while for general nilmanifolds it was established by Gorodnik and Spatzier
[
20
]
. There are also many prior results on exponential mixing. In the case of tori, the results are due to Lind
[
12
]
(for
l=1
and
n=2
), Dolgopyat
[
6
]
(for
l=1
and
n\geq 2
). For general nilmanifolds, the results were established by Gorodnik and Spatzier
[
20
]
(for
n\leq 4
) and Timothée and Varjú
[
27
]
(for
n\geq 4
).

For partially hyperbolic actions on (twisted) symmetric spaces
: It follows from the work of Dani
[
3
,
4
]
that, under mild assumptions, any partially hyperbolic one-parameter flow on
\mathcal{X}
satisfies the Kolmogorov property and is therefore mixing of all orders. Mozes
[
16
]
showed that for arbitrary measure-preserving actions of a connected semisimple Lie group
G
with finite center, mixing of order two implies mixing of all orders. Furthermore, using Ratner’s measure classification theorem, Starkov
[
26
]
proved that every mixing one-parameter flow on a finite-volume homogeneous space is mixing of all orders.

Although quantitative estimates for the mixing of order two have substantial history, the case of
n\geq 3
, under the assumption of strong spectral gap, was solved recently by Björklund, Einsiedler and Gorodnik
[
1
]
.

Difficulty in obtaining explicit rate
So far, the explicit mixing rate was only obtained when
n=2
, in the case of tori and symmetric spaces. But it seems that current methods cannot be used to obtain explicit mixing rate for any other cases.

2.
Main results for automorphisms of nilmanifolds

2.1.
Automorphisms of nilmanifolds

Let
N
be a simply connected nilpotent group and
\Gamma
a discrete cocompact
subgroup. We call the space
\mathcal{X}=N/\Gamma
a compact nilmanifolds. The nilmanifold
\mathcal{X}
carries a unique
N
-invariant
probability measure
\varrho
, locally given by the Haar measure of
N
. The group of automorphisms of
\mathcal{X}
is defined by

\displaystyle\text{Aut}(\mathcal{X})=\{A\in\text{Aut}(N):\,A(\Gamma)=\Gamma\}

and acts by measure-preserving transformations on
(\mathcal{X},\varrho)
. Set
\mathfrak{n}=\text{Lie}(N)
.

2.2.
Basic notations

1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an ergodic automorphism of
\mathcal{X}
. We have a decomposition of
\mathfrak{n}
into
d\mathcal{A}
-invariant subspaces:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{(1)}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}

where
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}^{(1)}}
has no eigenvalues that are roots of unity and
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}}
has only eigenvalues that are roots of unity.
We say that
\mathcal{A}
is of

•

rational type
if
\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}\neq 0
;

•

irrational type
if
\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}=0
.

2
.

Let
\chi_{1}<\cdots<\chi_{l}
be the Lyapunov exponents of
\mathcal{A}
and let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{L}_{l}

be the corresponding Lyapunov subspace decomposition. Let

\displaystyle W_{+}=\oplus_{\chi_{i}>0}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i},\quad W_{0}=\oplus_{\chi_{i}=0}\mathcal{L}_{i},\quad W_{-}=\oplus_{\chi_{i}<0}\mathcal{L}_{i}

and let

\displaystyle\chi=\min\{|\chi_{i}|:\,|\chi_{i}|\neq 0\}.

For any
z\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

\displaystyle V_{z}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle W_{-},&\text{if }z\geq 0,\\[2.84526pt]
W_{+},&\text{if }z<0;\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad V_{1,z}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle W_{+}\oplus W_{0},&\text{if }z\geq 0,\\[2.84526pt]
W_{-}\oplus W_{0},&\text{if }z<0.\end{cases}

3
.

Since
[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
is invariant under
d\mathcal{A}
,
d\mathcal{A}
descends to an automorphism
on
\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
, which we denote by
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the characteristic polynomial of
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
, and let
\mathfrak{p}=\prod_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{d_{i}}

be its prime decomposition over
\mathbb{Q}
. Then we have the corresponding splitting

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]=\oplus_{i=1}^{k_{0}}V_{i}

into rational
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
-invariant subspaces
V_{i}=\ker\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{d_{i}}(d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]})
.

Let
\rho_{i,\max}
be the maximal Lyapunov exponent and
\rho_{i,\min}
be the minimal Lyapunov exponent of
(d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]})|_{V_{i}}
respectively. Set

(2.2)

\displaystyle\rho=\min_{1\leq i\leq k_{0}}\max\{\rho_{i,\max},|\rho_{i,\min}|\}.

We point out that none of the eigenvalues of
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
is a root of unity if
\mathcal{A}
is ergodic (see Lemma
9.2
), then
\rho>0
.

2.3.
Results of a single automorphism

For any
z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
f_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n})=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{1}(\mathcal{A}^{z_{1}}x)\cdots f_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{z_{n}}x)d\varrho(x).

Here, for
z\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f\circ z
denotes the function
x\to f(\mathcal{A}^{z}x)
.

Set

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1,&\text{if }\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}\neq\{0\},\\[2.84526pt]
0,&\text{if }\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}=\{0\}.\end{cases}

Theorem 2.1
.

Let

\displaystyle f_{1}\in W^{\infty,\exp(V)}(L^{2}(\mathcal{X}))\quad\text{and}\quad f_{2}\in W^{\infty,\exp(V_{1})}(L^{2}(\mathcal{X})).

Then for any
z\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathfrak{m}(f_{1}\circ z,\,f_{2})-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\mathfrak{m}(f_{2})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{m,\epsilon}e^{-(\chi-\epsilon)\lvert z\rvert m}\|f_{1}\|_{\exp(V_{z}),m}\lVert f_{2}\rVert_{\exp(V_{1,z}),\,m\dim\mathfrak{n}}

(2.3)

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon}e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\rho|z|}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{\exp(V_{z}),\,\frac{1}{2}}\big\|f_{2}\big\|_{\exp(V_{1,z}),\,\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}.

Theorem 2.2
.

For any
m>0
, any
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and any
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

(1)

if
n=3
and
\mathfrak{m}(f_{i})=0
,
1\leq i\leq 3
, then

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},f_{3}\circ z_{3})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{m,\epsilon}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\chi-\epsilon)(\max_{1\leq i\neq j\leq 3}\lvert z_{i}-z_{j}\rvert)m}\big(\Pi_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i}\|_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}\big)

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\rho(\max_{1\leq i\neq j\leq 3}\lvert z_{i}-z_{j}\rvert)}\big(\Pi_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i}\|_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}\big).

(2)

if
n\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n})-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{m,\epsilon,n}e^{-(\chi-\epsilon)(\min_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\lvert z_{i}-z_{j}\rvert)m}\big(\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}\big)

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,n}e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\rho(\min_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\lvert z_{i}-z_{j}\rvert)}\big(\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}\big).

Remark 2.3
.

In all previous works, the estimates for higher order mixing (with
n\geq 3
) were expressed in terms of the minimal time separation between the indices.
In contrast, for
n=3
, we obtain a sharper estimate using the maximal time separation,
\max_{1\leq i\neq j\leq 3}|z_{i}-z_{j}|
.

This result is surprising because it was widely believed that super-exponential mixing occurs only for ergodic automorphisms on tori and solely in the 2-mixing case. In fact, even for tori, no known results establish super-exponential mixing for orders three or higher. Here, we demonstrate that on nilmanifolds, super-exponential decay can occur at all orders for non-rational automorphisms.

We now state the result for rational automorphisms. In order to state our result precisely, we need to
set up some notations.

Theorems
2.2
shows that for ergodic automorphisms on nilmanifolds, the mixing rate for any order is explicitly determined by Lyapunov exponents of
\mathcal{A}
. It is the first time the explicit mixing rate is obtained beyond the
n=2
case on tori. In previous works (e.g.,
[
20
]
and
[
27
]
),
the description of mixing rate relies on estimates from number theory. It seems unlikely to derive these estimates from dynamical properties of
\alpha_{\mathcal{Z}}
, such as its Lyapunov exponents.

Moreover, we note that the constant
C_{\eta,n}
(resp.
C_{n,\epsilon}
) depends explicitly on
\eta
and
n
(resp.
n
and
\epsilon
). In contrast, these constants in previous works are
indeterminate
when
n\geq 4
, which means that the proof of the theorem only shows that there exists a finite (and positive) value
of
C
for which the
n
-exponential mixing holds, without providing any means to determine its value.

2.4.
Results of general abelian automorphisms

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha:\mathbb{Z}^{l}\to\text{Aut}(\mathcal{X})
be an action of
\mathbb{Z}^{l}
by automorphisms on
\mathcal{X}
. Denote by
d\alpha
the induced action of
\alpha
on
\mathfrak{n}
, i.e., the linear part of
\alpha
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
, we have a decomposition of
\mathfrak{n}
into
d\alpha(z)
-invariant subspaces:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{(z,1)}+\mathfrak{n}^{(z,2)},

where
d\alpha(z)|_{\mathfrak{n}^{(z,1)}}
has no roots of unity and
d\alpha(z)|_{\mathfrak{n}^{(z,2)}}
only has roots of unity. Set

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}(d\alpha)=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}}\mathfrak{n}^{(z,2)}.

Set

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1,&\text{if }\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}\neq\{0\},\\[2.84526pt]
0,&\text{if }\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}=\{0\}.\end{cases}

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we use
\mathbb{S}_{n-1}
to denote the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and denote by
\mathfrak{u}
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{S}_{n}
.
Let
z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Then we define a map
L:\mathbb{Z}^{nl}\to\mathbb{S}_{nl-1}
as follows:
L(\mathfrak{z})=\mathfrak{z}/\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert
, where

\mathfrak{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{nl}
and
\mathfrak{z}/\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert\in\mathbb{S}_{nl-1}
. We define a subset
Z_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{nl}
such that

(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in Z
if
d_{\mathbb{S}_{nl-1}}(L((z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})),\bigcup_{\chi}L(\ker\chi))\geq\epsilon
. Is
Z_{\epsilon}
a large set of
\mathbb{Z}^{nl}
when
\epsilon
very small?

Indeed, one shows by lattice point counting that

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\#\{\mathbf{z}\in Z_{\epsilon}:\|\mathbf{z}\|\leq R\}}{\#\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\|\mathbf{z}\|\leq R\}}\;=\;\frac{\text{Vol}\bigl\{v\in S^{d-1}:d(v,\mathcal{H})\geq\epsilon\bigr\}}{\text{Vol}\bigl(S^{d-1}\bigr)}.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose there is
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
such that
d\alpha(z)|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
is ergodic. Then for any
n\geq 2
, there exists a set
\mathcal{R}_{n}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{nl}
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}\;=1

such that: for any
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and any
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
, there exists
\mathfrak{r}(\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert})>0
, where
\mathfrak{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
such that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert},n,m}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,n,\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Suppose there is
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
such that
d\alpha(z)|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
is ergodic. Then for any
n\geq 2
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
\mathfrak{r}(\varepsilon)>0
and a set
\mathcal{R}_{n,\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{nl}
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathcal{R}_{n,\varepsilon}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}\;\geq 1-\varepsilon

such that: for any
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and any
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{n,\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{n,m}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\varepsilon,n}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

3.
Main results for (Twisted) semisimple case

3.1.
(Twisted) semisimple case

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a
connected Lie group,
\Gamma
a (cocompact) torsion free lattice in

\mathbb{G}
. Then
\mathbb{G}
acts by left translation on the manifolds

\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{G}/\Gamma
.
\mathcal{X}
carries a unique
\mathbb{G}
-invariant
probability measure
\mu
, locally given by the Haar measure of
\mathbb{G}
. The corresponding action of
\mathbb{G}
on
L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mu)
is a unitary representation, which we denote by
\pi
. Let
G
be a connected semisimple Lie group of non-compact type with finite centre in

\mathbb{G}
, and let
V
be a simply connected nilpotent subgroup of

\mathbb{G}
together with a representation
\iota:G\to GL(V)
.

We suppose
\pi|_{G}
has a strong spectral gap, i.e., the restriction of
\pi
to each simple factor of
G
is isolated
from the trivial representation with respect to the Fell topology on the dual space. We now introduce some notations before we introduce the results.

1
.

Sobolev space
W^{s,H}(\mathcal{H})
: We denote by
W^{s,H}(\mathcal{H})
the Sobolev space of order
s
in the representation
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
, where the Sobolev norm is defined using differential operators corresponding only to the Lie algebra of a subgroup
H
(i.e., only derivatives along directions tangent to
H
are taken into account).

2
.

Decomposition of elements in
G\ltimes_{\iota}V
: For any
g\in G\ltimes_{\iota}V
, we can write
g=(c,v)
, where
c\in G
is the semisimple part and
v\in V
is the nilpotent part. We have a corresponding Jordan-Chevalley normal form decomposition of
3
commuting elements
c=s_{g}k_{g}n_{g}
where
s_{g}
is semisimple,
k_{g}
is compact and
n_{g}
is unipotent. Then there exists a maximal split torus
A
of
G
, such that
s_{g}\subseteq A
.

3
.

Cartan decomposition and Root system
: Let
A
be a maximal split torus of
G
and
A^{+}
the closed positive Weyl chamber of
A
such
that the Cartan decomposition
G=KA^{+}K
holds. We fix a
left-invariant Riemannian metric
d
on
G
which is bi-invariant under
K
.

Denote by
\Phi
the set of non-multipliable roots of
A
and by
\Phi^{+}

the set of positive roots in
\Phi
.
The group
\mathbb{G}
contains a connected
split subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{0}
such that we have
A\subset\mathbb{G}_{0}
and
\Phi=\Phi(A,\mathbb{G}_{0})
.

4
.

Strongly orthogonal system of
\Phi
: A subset
\mathcal{S}
of
\Phi^{+}
is called a strongly orthogonal system
of
\Phi
if any two distinct elements
\gamma
and
\beta
of
\mathcal{S}
are strongly orthogonal, that is, neither
of
\gamma\pm\beta
belongs to
\Phi
. A strongly orthogonal system
\mathcal{S}
is called maximal if the coefficient of each simple
root in the formal sum
\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{S}}\gamma
is not less than the one in

\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{O}}\gamma
for any strongly
orthogonal system
\mathcal{O}
of
\Phi
.

5
.

Basic subgroups
: For each
\gamma\in\Phi
choose non-trivial
U_{\gamma}
inside the one-dimensional root space of
\gamma
in
\text{Lie}(\mathbb{G}_{0})
. Let
u_{\gamma}
denote the corresponding one-dimensional root subgroup generated by
U_{\gamma}
. Then there exists

U_{\gamma^{-1}}
inside the one-dimensional root space of
\gamma^{-1}
such that
\{U_{\gamma},U_{\gamma^{-1}},X_{\gamma}=[U_{\gamma},U_{\gamma^{-1}}]\}
forms an
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
-triple. Let
x_{\gamma}
denote the one-dimensional subgroup generated by
X_{\gamma}
.
Denote by
\mathcal{V}_{\gamma}
the subgroup generated by
x_{\gamma}
and
u_{\gamma^{-1}}
.

For a strongly orthogonal system
\mathcal{S}
, set

\displaystyle S(\mathcal{S})=\Pi_{\gamma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{V}_{\gamma}\quad\text{ and }\quad u(\mathcal{S})=\Pi_{\gamma\in\mathcal{S}}u_{\gamma}.

Note
. For any
a\in A^{+}
,
U_{\gamma}
lies in the unstable subspace of
\text{Ad}_{a}
and
U_{\gamma^{-1}}
and
X_{\gamma}
lie in the weak stable subspace. This shows that cosets of
U(\mathcal{S})
lies in the unstable foliaitons of the left translations of
\pi(a)
and
S(\mathcal{S})
lies in weak stable foliaitons.

6
.

Spectral gap
: For each
\gamma\in\Phi
let
u_{\gamma}
denote the corresponding one-dimensional root subgroup generated by
U_{\gamma}
in
\mathbb{G}_{0}
. Let
H_{\gamma}
denote the closed subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{0}
generated by
u_{\pm\gamma}
. Then we have
\text{Lie}(H_{\gamma})=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Since
\pi
has strong spectral gap, for any
\gamma\in\Phi
,

\pi|_{H_{\gamma}}
has a spectral gap, which we denote by
\mu_{\gamma}
. Let

\displaystyle\nu_{\gamma}:=\begin{cases}\sqrt{1-4\mu_{\gamma}}\qquad&\text{if }\mu_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{4},\\
0\qquad&\text{if }\mu_{\gamma}\geq\frac{1}{4}.\end{cases}

7
.

Let
a\in A
,
\mathcal{S}
be a strongly orthogonal system of
A
and
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small. Define:

\displaystyle r_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S})=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{S}}(\frac{1+\nu_{\gamma}}{2}+\epsilon),\qquad\bar{r}(\mathcal{S})=\lfloor\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{S}}(\frac{1+\nu_{\gamma}}{2})\rfloor+1+|\mathcal{S}|,

\displaystyle r(\mathcal{S})=\lfloor\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{S}}(\frac{1+\nu_{\gamma}}{2})\rfloor+1

\displaystyle\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},a)=\Pi_{\gamma\in\mathcal{S}}\gamma(a^{+})^{\frac{-1+\nu_{\gamma}}{2}+\epsilon},

where for each
a\in A
, there exists an element
w
inside the Weyl group such that
a^{+}=w^{-1}aw\in A^{+}
.

For any
g_{i}\in\mathbb{G}
and
f_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(f_{1}\circ g_{1},f_{2}\circ g_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ g_{n})=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{1}(g_{1}x)\cdots f_{n}(g_{n}x)d\mu(x).

Here, for
g\in\mathbb{G}
,
f\circ g
denotes the function
x\to f(gx)
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a strongly orthogonal system of
A
. Suppose
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
is a unitary representation of
G
with a strong spectral gap. For any
a\in A
and any
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
, if

\displaystyle\xi_{1}\in W^{r_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S}),\,u(\mathcal{S})}(\mathcal{H})\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{2}\in W^{r_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S})+|\mathcal{S}|,\,S(\mathcal{S})}(\mathcal{H}),

then we have

\displaystyle\big|\langle\rho(a)\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\rangle\big|\leq C_{\epsilon}\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},a)\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert_{u(\mathcal{S}),r_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S})}\,\big\|\xi_{2}\big\|_{S(\mathcal{S}),r_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S})+|\mathcal{S}|}.

Starting with Harish-Chandra’s research programme (summarised in the
monographs [42, 43]), properties of matrix coefficients for representations of semisimple Lie
groups have been extensively studied. In particular, important works by Borel and
Wallach [4], Cowling [6], Howe [17], Li and Zhu [22, 23], Moore [29], and Oh [32, 33] provide explicit estimates on matrix coefficients for semisimple groups. All these estimates are for
K
-finite vectors, and the optimal rate was obtained by Oh
[
23
]
.
Katok and Spatzier extended this optimal rate to smooth vectors
[
18
]
; however, in their work the required Sobolev regularity is determined abstractly by

K
and tends to be large.

In contrast, our result also obtains the same optimal decay rate as in
[
23
]
, but with a key difference in the Sobolev regularity requirements:
only partial Sobolev regularity is required for the vector
\xi_{2}
, and the regularity conditions for both
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are explicitly specified.
The partial regularity plays a crucial role in obtaining explicit exponential decay rates for mixing of all orders.

Remark 3.2
.

We explain the meaning the Sobolev norms in Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose
g\in G\ltimes_{\iota}V
and
A
is a maximal split torus of
G
such that
s_{g}\subseteq A
.
Let
\mathcal{S}
be a maximal strongly orthogonal system of
A
. For any
n\geq 2
, For any
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C_{c}^{\bar{r}}(\mathcal{X})
and any
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

(1)

if
n=3
and
\mathfrak{m}(f_{i})=0
,
1\leq i\leq 3
, then

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ g^{z_{1}},f_{2}\circ g^{z_{2}},f_{3}\circ g^{z_{3}}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{g,\epsilon}\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},s_{g})^{\max_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}|z_{i}-z_{j}|}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}}}\lVert f_{2}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}}}\lVert f_{3}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}}}.

If
g=s_{g}\in A
, then
C_{g,\epsilon}
can be taken only dependent on
\epsilon
;

(2)

if
n\geq 2
and
g=s_{g}\in A
, then for any
t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ g^{t_{1}},f_{2}\circ g^{t_{2}},\cdots,f_{n}\circ g^{t_{n}}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},s_{g})^{\min_{1\leq i,j\leq k}|t_{i}-t_{j}|}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}}}\cdots\lVert f_{n}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}}};

(3)

if
n\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ g^{z_{1}},f_{2}\circ g^{z_{2}},\cdots,f_{n}\circ g^{z_{n}}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{g,\epsilon}\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},s_{g})^{\min_{1\leq i,j\leq k}|z_{i}-z_{j}|}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}}}\cdots\lVert f_{n}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}}}.

Next, we state our results for higher order mixing.

Results of general abelian actions.
We state result if
\mathcal{Z}
is a general abelian subgroup in
G\ltimes V
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Suppose
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\in C_{c}^{\bar{r}}(\mathcal{X})
, then

(1)

we have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}(f_{1}\circ g_{1},\dots,f_{k}\circ g_{k})-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{k})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{k,\mathcal{Z},\epsilon}\,\min_{1\leq i\neq j\leq k}\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},s_{g_{i}g_{j}^{-1}})^{\frac{1}{(k-1)|\mathcal{S}|}}\,\Pi_{i=1}^{k}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}(\mathcal{S})}}.

(2)

If
k=3
and
\mathfrak{m}(f_{i})=0
,
1\leq i\leq 3
, then there exist
1\leq i_{1}\neq i_{2}\leq 3
with

\displaystyle d(s_{g_{i_{1}}},s_{g_{i_{2}}})=\max_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}d(s_{g_{i}},s_{g_{j}})

(see (
3
) of Section
3.1
) such that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}(f_{1}\circ g_{1},f_{2}\circ g_{2},f_{3}\circ g_{3})\Big|\leq C_{\mathcal{Z},\epsilon}\,\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},s_{g_{i_{1}}g_{i_{2}}^{-1}})^{\frac{1}{2|\mathcal{S}|}}\,\Pi_{i=1}^{3}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{\bar{r}(\mathcal{S})}}.

3.2.
Proof strategy

4.
Preliminaries

Constants
C
,
C_{r}
, and
C_{i,n}
may change from line to line but depend only on the indicated parameters.

4.1.
Preliminaries on unitary representation theory

Let
\rho
be a unitary representation of a Lie group
S
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{S}
on a Hilbert space
H_{\rho}
. We call a vector
\xi\in H_{\rho}

C^{\infty}
for
S
if the map

\displaystyle\gamma\to\rho(\gamma)\xi

is a
C^{\infty}
function from
S
to
H_{\rho}
. We use
W^{\infty}(H_{\rho})
to denote the set of
C^{\infty}
vectors. The derived representation
\rho_{*}
of
\rho
is the Lie algebra representation of
\mathfrak{S}
on
H_{\rho}
defined as follows. For every
X\in\mathfrak{S}
,

(4.1)

\rho_{*}(X):=\operatorname{strong{-}}\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\rho(\exp tX)-I}{t}.

For any
v\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
, we also write
Xv:=\rho_{*}(X)v
for simplicity.

It can be shown that the derived representation
\rho_{*}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{S}
on the Hilbert space
H_{\rho}
is essentially skew-adjoint in the following sense: For every element
X\in\mathfrak{S}
, the operator
\rho_{*}(X)
is essentially skew-adjoint. Moreover, these operators share a common invariant core, which is the subspace
W^{\infty}(H_{\rho})\subset H_{\rho}
of
C^{\infty}
vectors.

The subspace
W^{\infty}(H_{\rho})\subset H_{\rho}
is endowed with the Fréchet
C^{\infty}
topology, that is, the topology defined by the family of seminorms

\left\{\,\|\cdot\|_{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m}}\;\middle|\;m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathfrak{S}\,\right\}

defined as follows:

\|\xi\|_{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m}}:=\left\|\,\rho_{*}(v_{1})\rho_{*}(v_{2})\cdots\rho_{*}(v_{m})\xi\,\right\|,\quad\text{for }\xi\in C^{\infty}(H_{\rho}).

For simplicity, we often write

\displaystyle v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{m}\xi:=\rho_{*}(v_{1})\rho_{*}(v_{2})\cdots\rho_{*}(v_{m})\xi,\quad\text{for }\xi\in C^{\infty}(H_{\rho}),

which streamlines notation when dealing with multiple elements and operations.

The space of
Schwartz distributions
for the representation
\rho
is defined as the dual space of
C^{\infty}(H_{\rho})
(endowed with the Fréchet
C^{\infty}
topology). The space of Schwartz distributions for the representation
\rho
will be denoted
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(H_{\rho})
.

The representation
\rho_{*}
extends in a canonical way to a representation (denoted by the same symbol) of the enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{S})
of
\mathfrak{S}
on the Hilbert space
H_{\rho}
. Let
\Delta_{S}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{S})
be a left-invariant second-order, positive elliptic operator on
S
fixed once for all. For example, the operator

\displaystyle\Delta_{S}=-\left(v_{1}^{2}+\cdots+v_{d}^{2}\right)

where
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{S}
as a vector space.

For each
\alpha>0
, let

W^{\alpha,S}(H_{\rho})\;=\;\mathrm{Dom}\bigl((I+\Delta_{S})^{\alpha/2}\bigr)\;\subset\;H_{\rho}

be the Sobolev space of order
\alpha
with respect to the group
S
on the Hilbert space
H_{\rho}
. Equip it with the full norm

\|\xi\|_{S,\alpha,H_{\rho}}\;=\;\bigl\|(I+\Delta_{S})^{\alpha/2}\,\xi\bigr\|_{H_{\rho}},\qquad\xi\in W^{\alpha,S}(H_{\rho}).

When
S
and
H_{\rho}
are clear from context, we may abbreviate

\|\xi\|_{S,\alpha,H_{\rho}}=\|\xi\|_{S,\alpha}=\|\xi\|_{\alpha}.

If instead we restrict to a subgroup
P\subset S
, we write

W^{\alpha,P}(H_{\rho})=\mathrm{Dom}\bigl((I+\Delta_{P})^{\alpha/2}\bigr),

with norm

\|\xi\|_{P,\alpha,H_{\rho}}=\bigl\|(I+\Delta_{P})^{\alpha/2}\,\xi\bigr\|_{H_{\rho}},

and similarly drop subscripts when no ambiguity arises.

Let also
W^{-\alpha}(H_{\rho})\subset\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(H_{\rho})
be the dual Hilbert space of
W^{\alpha}(H_{\rho})
. Then
W^{\infty}(H_{\rho})
is the projective limit of the spaces
W^{\alpha}(H_{\rho})
(and consequently
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(H_{\rho})
is the inductive limit of
W^{-\alpha}(H_{\rho})
) as
\alpha\to+\infty
. A distribution
\mathcal{D}\in W^{-\alpha}(H_{\rho})
will be called a distribution of order at most
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

4.2.
Unitary dual of
SL(2,\mathbb{R})

We recall the conclusions in
[
8
]
. We choose as generators for
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
the elements

(4.2)

\displaystyle X=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&0\\
0&-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad U=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad V=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

The
Casimir
operator is then given by

\displaystyle\Box:=-X^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(UV+VU),

which generates the center of the enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
. The Casimir operator
\Box

acts as a constant a constant
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{-n^{2}+n\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\}
on the Hilbert space of each irreducible unitary representation, and its value classifies all nontrivial irreducible unitary representations according to three different types. Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
be the Hilbert space of an irreducible unitary representation on which the Casimir operator takes the value
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{-n^{2}+n\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\}
. The representation is said to belong to:

•

the principal series representations if
\mu\geq\frac{1}{4}
,

•

the complementary series representations if
0<\mu<\frac{1}{4}
,

•

and the discrete series representations if
\mu\leq 0
.

Any unitary representation
(\pi,\mathcal{H})
of
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
is decomposed into a direct integral (see
[
8
]
and
[
14
]
)

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\int_{\oplus}\mathcal{H}_{\mu}ds(\mu)\quad\text{and}\quad\omega=\int_{\oplus}\omega_{\mu}ds(\mu)\quad\forall\,\omega\in\mathcal{H}

with respect to a positive Stieltjes measure
ds(r)
over the spectrum
\sigma(\Box)
. The
Casimir operator acts as the constant
u\in\sigma(\Box)
on every Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
. The
representations induced on
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
do not need to be irreducible. In fact,
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
is in general
the direct sum of an (at most countable) number of unitary representations equal
to the spectral multiplicity of
\mu\in\sigma(\Box)
. We say that
\pi
has a spectral gap (of
\mu_{0}
)
if
\mu_{0}>0
and
s((0,\mu_{0}))=0
. Let

\nu_{0}\in[0,1)
be defined as

(4.4)

\displaystyle\nu_{0}:=\begin{cases}\sqrt{1-4\mu_{0}}\qquad&\text{if }\mu_{0}<\frac{1}{4},\\
0\qquad&\text{if }\mu_{0}\geq\frac{1}{4}.\end{cases}

4.3.
Preliminaries on nilpotent real Lie algebra

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be a
k
-step nilpotent real Lie algebra,
k\geq 2
. Let
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq k
denote the descending central series of
\mathfrak{n}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{1}=\mathfrak{n},\,\,\mathfrak{n}_{2}=[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}],\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{j}=[\mathfrak{n}_{j-1},\mathfrak{n}],\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{k}\subset Z(\mathfrak{n})

where
Z(\mathfrak{n})
is the center of
\mathfrak{n}
. Set
\mathfrak{n}_{k+1}=\{0\}
.

Let
N
be the connected and simply connected nilpotent Lie group with
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{n}
. The corresponding Lie subgroups
N_{1}=\exp\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
N_{j}=\exp\mathfrak{n}_{j}=[N_{j-1},N]
,
j\geq 2

form the descending central series of
N
. Let
\Gamma
be a lattice in
N
. Set
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{j}=[\Gamma_{j-1},\Gamma]
,
j\geq 2
.

The nilmanifold
\mathcal{X}=N/\Gamma
carries a unique
N
-invariant
probability measure
\mu
, locally given by the Haar measure of
N
. We use
\pi
to denote the regular representation of
N
on
L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\varrho)
.

4.3.1.
Malcev basis

Let
E_{1}^{1},E_{2}^{1},\cdots,E_{n_{1}}^{1},E_{1}^{2},\cdots,E_{n_{2}}^{2},\cdots,E_{1}^{k},\cdots,E_{n_{k}}^{k}
, a
Malcev basis for
\mathfrak{n}
through the descending central series
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and strongly
based at
\Gamma
, that is a basis of
\mathfrak{n}
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

if we drop the first
p
elements of the basis we obtain a basis of a
subalgebra of codimension
p
of
\mathfrak{n}
.

(2)

If we set
\mathcal{E}^{j}=\{E_{1}^{j},\cdots,E_{n_{j}}^{j}\}
, the elements of the set
\mathcal{E}^{j}\cup\mathcal{E}^{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{E}^{k}
form a basis of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
.

(3)

every element of
\Gamma
can be written as a product

\displaystyle\exp m_{1}^{1}E_{1}^{1}\cdots\exp m_{n_{1}}^{1}E_{n_{1}}^{1}\cdots\exp m_{1}^{k}E_{1}^{k}\cdots\exp m_{n_{k}}^{k}E_{n_{k}}^{k}

with integral coefficients
m^{j}_{i}
.

The lattice
\Gamma
determines a rational structure on
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
for each
j
.
Specifically, the elements of the set
\mathcal{E}^{j}\cup\mathcal{E}^{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{E}^{k}
generate a
\mathbb{Z}^{\dim\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
-lattice in
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
.
We say that a subspace
E\subset\mathfrak{n}_{j}
is
\Gamma
-
rational
if it admits a basis of integer combinations of these
E_{i}^{j}
’s. More precisely, if there are integer vectors
W_{i}
(with respect to the Malcev basis),
1\leq i\leq\dim E
such that
E
is spanned by
\{W_{1},\cdots,W_{\dim E}\}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{X}
, and let
d\mathcal{A}
be its linear part. Clearly,
d\mathcal{A}|_{E}
preserves these
E_{i}^{j}
basis of
\mathfrak{n}
. If a
\Gamma
-rational subspace
E
is invariant under
d\mathcal{A}
,
then
d\mathcal{A}|_{E}
preserves the lattice spanned by
\{W_{1},\cdots,W_{\dim E}\}
. Thus,
d\mathcal{A}|_{E}

is represented (in a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
E
) by some matrix
M_{\mathcal{A}}\in GL(\dim E,\mathbb{Z})
.

4.3.2.
Diophantine subspace of
\Gamma
-rational subspace

Suppose
E
is a
\Gamma
-rational subspace. Choose an integer basis
\{W_{1},\cdots,W_{\dim E}\}
of
E
. Then for any
w\in E
there exists
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{\dim E})\in\mathbb{R}^{\dim E}

such that

\displaystyle w=w_{1}W_{1}+\cdots+w_{\dim E}W_{\dim E}.

Set

(4.5)

\displaystyle\Delta_{E}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}W_{i}^{2}

Then
\Delta_{E}
, the Laplacian is an elliptic element of the universal enveloping algebra of
E
.

For any
m=(m_{1},\cdots,m_{\dim E})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\dim E}
set

\displaystyle m\cdot w=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim E}m_{i}w_{i}.

We say that a subspace
V\subset E
is a
Diophantine subspace
if for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\dim E}
and any basis
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim V}\}
of
V
, we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\dim V}|m\cdot v_{i}|\geq C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim V}}\lVert m\rVert^{-\dim E},

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is induced by the Euclidean distance.

It is clear that the definition of Diophantine subspace is not dependent on the choice of an integer basis of
E
.

4.3.3.
Coboundary for the horocycle
flow of
SL(2,\mathbb{R})

For the classical horocycle flow defined by the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
-matrix
U=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
, Flaminio and Forni made a detailed study in
[
8
]
.

Theorem 4.1
.

(Theorem 4.1 of
[
8
]
) Let
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
be a unitary representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
without
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-fixed vectors. If
\pi
has a spectral gap of
\mu_{0}
and
s>\frac{1+\nu_{0}}{2}
, where
\nu_{0}
is defined as in (
4.4
), then there exists a constant
C_{\nu_{0},s,t}>0
such that for any
\xi\in W^{s}(\mathcal{H})
,

•

if
t<-\frac{1+\nu_{0}}{2}
, or

•

if
t<s-1
and
\mathcal{D}(\xi)=0
for any
U
-invariant distribution
\mathcal{D}
of Sobolev order
s

then the equation

\displaystyle U\omega=\xi

has a solution
\omega\in W^{t}(\mathcal{H})
which satisfies the Sobolev estimate

\|\omega\|_{t}\leq C_{\nu_{0},s,t}\|\xi\|_{s}.

4.3.4.
Coboundary for the Diophantine nilflow on
N/\Gamma

Let
V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}
denote the linear space spanned by vectors in
\mathcal{E}^{1}
(see Section
4.3.1
). We use
\mathfrak{p}
to denote natural projection from
\mathfrak{n}
to
\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
. We say that
\bar{X}\in V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}
is a Diophantine vector of
V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}
if
\bar{X}
spans a one-dimensional Diophantine subspace of
V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}
(see Section
4.3.2
). We say that
X\in\mathfrak{n}
is a
Diophantine vector for
\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
if there is a Diophantine vector
\bar{X}
of
V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}
such that
\mathfrak{p}(X)=\mathfrak{p}(\bar{X})
.

Theorem 4.2
.

(Theorem 1.3 of
[
7
]
)
Let
\mathcal{H}
denote the set of functions in
L^{2}(N/\Gamma)
with
0
averages.

(1)

If
\beta<-\frac{1}{2}
, there exists a linear bounded operator

G_{X}:W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})\to W^{\beta}(\mathcal{H})

such that, for all
f\in W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, the cohomological equation

\displaystyle Xu=f

has a distributional weak solution
u=G_{X}f
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{I}_{X}
denote the set of
X
-invariant distributions in
W^{-\infty}(\mathcal{H})
. Define

W_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})=\{f\in W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}):\mathcal{D}(f)=0\text{ for all }\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{I}_{X}\}.

Then there exists a linear bounded operator

G_{X}:W_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})\to W^{\beta}(\mathcal{H})

such that, for all
f\in W_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, the function
u=G_{X}f
is a solution of the cohomological equation
Xu=f.

4.3.5.
Change of spectrum

We assume notations in Section
1.2
.
We have the following result:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
t_{1},\cdots,t_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}=|\mathfrak{u}_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\mathfrak{u}_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\mathfrak{u}_{m}|^{t_{m}}.

Then:

(1)

For any
\xi,\,\eta\in W^{\infty,\,S_{1}}(\mathcal{H})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{T}\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle\xi,\mathcal{T}\eta\rangle.

(2)

for any
t_{1},\cdots,t_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{T}\xi\big\|=\big\|\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{t_{1}}\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{t_{2}}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{m}^{t_{m}}\xi\big\|.

(3)

For any
h\in H
, we have

\displaystyle\pi(h^{-1})\mathcal{T}\pi(h)=a_{1}^{t_{1}}\cdots a_{m}^{t_{m}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{m}|^{t_{m}},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{\|\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}\|},\quad\text{and}\quad a_{i}=\|\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}\|,\quad 1\leq i\leq m.

The proof is left for Section
B
.

Remark 4.4
.

(
1
) shows that the operator
\mathcal{T}
is symmetric (self-adjoint on its domain).

(
3
) shows that conjugation by
\pi(h)
transforms the spectral variables by scaling via

a_{i}=\|\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}\|

and replaces
\mathfrak{u}_{i}
with its normalized conjugate
\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{i}
.

5.
Study of codicological equations, semisimple case

Suppose
S
is Lie group with
\text{Lie}(S)=\underbrace{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})}_{n}
. Let
S_{i}
denote the subgroup of
S
with its Lie algebra being the
i
-th copy of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Let
X_{i}
,
U_{i}
and
V_{i}
(see (
4.2
)) denote the corresponding element in the
i
-th copy of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Set

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}=(I-X_{i}^{2}-V_{i}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(\pi,\mathcal{H})
be a unitary representation of
S
. If for any
1\leq i\leq n
, we have:

(1)

\pi|_{S_{i}}
has a spectral gap of
\mu_{i}
;

(2)

\pi|_{S_{i}}
has no nontrivial fixed vectors;

(3)

1>r_{i}>\frac{1+\nu_{i}}{2}
, where
\nu_{i}
is defined as in (
4.4
) for
\mu_{i}
;

(4)

0<p_{i}<1
such that
r_{i}+p_{i}<1
,

then for any

\displaystyle\xi\in\mathcal{H}\quad\text{ with }\quad\Sigma_{n}^{\frac{r_{n}+1}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}\xi\in\mathcal{H},

the equation

\displaystyle|U_{1}|^{p_{1}}|U_{2}|^{p_{2}}\cdots|U_{n}|^{p_{n}}\omega=\xi

has a unique solution
\omega\in\mathcal{H}
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\omega\rVert\leq C_{\nu,\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}\big\|\Sigma_{n}^{\frac{r_{n}+1}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}\xi\big\|,

where
\nu=(\nu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{n})
,
\mathfrak{r}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{n})
and
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\cdots,p_{n})
.

5.1.
Direct decompositions of Sobolev space

For any Lie group
S
of type
I
and its unitary representation
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
, there is a decomposition of
\rho
into a direct integral

\displaystyle\rho=\int_{Z}\pi_{z}d\mu(z)

of irreducible unitary representations for some measure space
(Z,\mu)
(we refer to

[
31
, Chapter 2.3]
or
[
13
]
for more detailed account for the direct integral theory). All the operators in the enveloping algebra are decomposable with respect to the direct integral decomposition. Hence there exists for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
an induced direct
decomposition of the Sobolev spaces

\displaystyle W^{s}(\mathcal{H})=\int_{Z}W^{s}(\mathcal{H}_{z})d\mu(z)

with respect to the measure
d\mu(z)
.

The existence of the direct integral decompositions allows us to reduce our analysis of the
cohomological equation to irreducible unitary representations. This point of view is
essential for our purposes.

5.2.
Self-adjoint operators

Let
H
be a Hilbert space, and let

\mathcal{P}
be an unbounded, self-adjoint linear operator with a dense domain
\text{Dom}(\mathcal{P})\subset H
. Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is strictly positive, meaning there is
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{P}\vartheta\rVert\geq c\lVert\vartheta\rVert,\qquad\forall\,\vartheta\in\text{Dom}(\mathcal{P}),

then there is a regular Borel measure
\tau
on
[c,\infty)
such that
\mathcal{P}
is unitarily equivalent to multiplication by
\lambda
on

(5.1)

\displaystyle\int_{[c,\infty)}H_{\lambda}d\tau(\lambda).

More precisely, we have
a direct integral decomposition:

\displaystyle\vartheta=\int_{[c,\infty)}\vartheta_{\lambda}d\tau(\lambda),\qquad\forall\,\vartheta\in H.

Furthermore, for any (measurable) function
g:[c,\infty)\to\mathbb{C}
, the functional calculus gives

\displaystyle g(\mathcal{P})\vartheta=\int_{[c,\infty)}g(\lambda)\vartheta_{\lambda}d\tau(\lambda),\qquad\forall\,\vartheta\in\text{Dom}(g(\mathcal{P})),

where

\displaystyle\text{Dom}(g(\mathcal{P}))=\{\vartheta\in H:\int_{[c,\infty)}|g(\lambda)|^{2}\lVert\vartheta_{\lambda}\rVert^{2}d\tau(\lambda)<\infty\}.

A symmetric operator
\mathcal{T}
is said to be essentially self-adjoint if the closure
\bar{\mathcal{T}}
of

\mathcal{T}
is self-adjoint. An essentially self-adjoint operator is “almost as good” a self-adjoint operator, since one obtains a self-adjoint operator simply by taking the closure.
If such an operator
\mathcal{T}
is strictly positive, then its closure
\bar{\mathcal{T}}
is also strictly positive. Moreover,
\bar{\mathcal{T}}^{\!r}
is also self-adjoint for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, whenever we speak of the spectral decomposition (
5.1
) of
\mathcal{T}^{r}
(where
\mathcal{T}
is an essentially self-adjoint, strictly positive operator
\mathcal{T}
),
we are really referring to the spectral decomposition of

\bar{\mathcal{T}}^{r}
.

5.3.
A Fourier model for
SL(2,\mathbb{R})

We use the representation parameter

\nu:=\sqrt{1-\mu}\,,

for convenience, and we denote the real part of
\nu
by
{\operatorname{Re}}\nu
.

The Fourier transform for functions in the line model
H_{\mu}
of the principal or complementary series is defined by

\hat{f}(\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)e^{-\sqrt{-1}\lambda x}dx\,.

Following
[
10
]
, for functions in the upper half-plane model of the discrete series or mock discrete series,
also denoted by
H_{\mu}
, the Fourier transform is given by

\hat{f}(\lambda):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x+\sqrt{-1})e^{-\sqrt{-1}\lambda(x+\sqrt{-1})}dx\,.

Cauchy’s theorem implies that
\hat{f}
is supported on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
when
f\in H_{\mu}^{\infty}
(see Lemma 3.15 of
[
10
]
).

By direct computation from the vector field formulas given in Appendix
A
,
we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\hat{U}=-i\lambda,\quad\hat{X}=(\nu-1)-2\lambda\frac{d}{d\lambda},\quad\hat{V}=i\left((\nu-1)\frac{d}{d\lambda}-\lambda\frac{d^{2}}{d\lambda^{2}}\right).

Moreover, for all cases (i.e., principal, complementary, discrete, and mock discrete series), Lemma 3.1 and Lemma 3.16 of
[
9
]
give a
constant
C_{{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}>0
such that

\|f\|^{2}=C_{{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{f}(\lambda)|^{2}|\lambda|^{-{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}d\lambda\,.

5.4.
New study of cohomological equation over horocycle flow

Let
P
be a Lie group
\text{Lie}(P)=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
be a unitary representation of
P
. Let
U\subset\text{Lie}(P)
be as defined in (
4.2
).

Proposition 5.2
.

Assume the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

\rho|_{P}
has a spectral gap of
\mu_{0}
and
\rho
has no
P
-fixed vectors,

(2)

\frac{1+\nu_{0}}{2}<r<1
, where
\nu_{0}
is defined as in (
4.4
).

Then for any
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
with
(I-V^{2}-X^{2})^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\xi
and any
0<p<1
such that
r+p<1
, the fractional equation

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega=\xi;

has a solution
\omega\in\mathcal{H}
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\omega\rVert\leq C_{f,\nu_{0},r,p}\big\|(I-V^{2}-X^{2})^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\xi\big\|.

Lemma 5.3
.

Assume conditions (
1
) and (
2
) in Proposition
5.2
are satisfied. Then for any
\xi\in W^{r}(\mathcal{H})
,
\psi\in W^{1+r}(\mathcal{H})
and
any
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle\rho(\exp(tU))\psi,\,\xi\big\rangle\Big|\leq C_{\nu_{0},r}(|t|+1)^{-2(1-r)}\|\xi\|_{r}\big\|\psi\big\|_{1+r}.

Proof.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the matrix
u_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\[4.0pt]
0&1\end{pmatrix}

has the KAM decomposition

u_{t}=k_{1}ak_{2},

with

k_{i}\in SO(2)\quad\text{and}\quad a=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\operatorname{arcsinh}(t/2)}&0\\[5.0pt]
0&e^{-\operatorname{arcsinh}(t/2)}\end{pmatrix},

Let
W
stand for
U
(resp.
V
) if
t>0
(resp.
t<0
). By Theorem
4.1
, the equation

(5.3)

\displaystyle W\eta=\rho(k_{1}^{-1})\xi

has a solution
\eta\in W^{-r}(\mathcal{H})
,
1>r>\frac{1+\nu_{0}}{2}
, which satisfies the Sobolev estimate

(5.4)

\displaystyle\|\eta\|_{-r}\leq C_{\nu_{0},r}\|\rho(k_{1}^{-1})\xi\|_{r}\leq C_{\nu_{0},r,1}\|\xi\|_{r}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle\rho(\exp(tU))\psi,\,\xi\big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\big\langle\rho(a)\rho(k_{2})\psi,\,\rho(k_{1}^{-1})\xi\big\rangle\Big|=\Big|\big\langle\rho(a)\rho(k_{2})\psi,\,W\eta\big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle=e^{-2\operatorname{arcsinh}(t/2)}\Big|\big\langle\rho(a)\big(W\rho(k_{2})\psi\big),\,\eta\big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\operatorname{arcsinh}(t/2)}C_{\nu_{0},r,1}\|\xi\|_{r}\big\|\rho(a)\big(W\rho(k_{2})\psi\big)\big\|_{r}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\operatorname{arcsinh}(t/2)}e^{2\operatorname{arcsinh}(t/2)r}C_{\nu_{0},r,2}\|\xi\|_{r}\big\|W\rho(k_{2})\psi\big\|_{r}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\nu_{0},r,3}(|t|+1)^{-2(1-r)}\|\xi\|_{r}\big\|\psi\big\|_{1+r}.

∎

Lemma 5.4
.

In
H_{\mu}
, for any
\xi\in W^{r+1}(\mathcal{H})
,
\frac{1+\nu}{2}<r<1
, then for any
0<p<1
such that
r+p<1
, the fractional equation

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega=\xi;

has a solution
\omega\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}\leq C\Gamma(1-2p)p^{-1}\big\|\xi\big\|^{2}+C_{\nu,r}\Gamma(1-2p)(1-r-p)\big\|\xi\big\|_{1+r}^{2}.

Proof.

We consider the Fourier model in Section
5.3
. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle\pi_{\mu}(\exp(tU))\xi,\,\xi\big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle=C_{{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\lambda t}|\xi(\lambda)|^{2}|\lambda|^{-{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}d\lambda=\hat{\theta}(t)

where

\displaystyle\theta(\lambda)=C_{{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}|\xi(\lambda)|^{2}|\lambda|^{-{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}.

It is clear that

(5.5)

\displaystyle|\hat{\theta}(t)|\leq\lVert\xi\rVert^{2},\qquad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}.

By Lemma
5.2
, we have the decay estimate

(5.6)

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}(t)\leq C_{\nu,r}(|t|+1)^{-2(1-r)}\big\|\xi\big\|_{1+r}^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{\xi(\lambda)}{|\lambda|^{p}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega=|\lambda|^{p}\omega=\xi.

Next, we estimate
\lVert\omega\rVert
. We have

\displaystyle\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\xi(\lambda)}{|\lambda|^{p}}\right\|^{2}=C_{{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi(\lambda)|^{2}|\lambda|^{-2p}|\lambda|^{-{\operatorname{Re}}\nu}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\theta(\lambda)|\lambda|^{-2p}d\lambda\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{\theta}(t)2\Gamma(1-2p)\sin(\pi p)|t|^{2p-1}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{|t|\leq 1}\hat{\theta}(t)2\Gamma(1-2p)\sin(\pi p)|t|^{2p-1}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{|t>1|}\hat{\theta}(t)2\Gamma(1-2p)\sin(\pi p)|t|^{2p-1}dt.

Here in
(1)
we note that the Fourier inversion formula for
|\lambda|^{-2p}

only holds in the sense of distributions, which is when
0<p<\frac{1}{2}
. Since we assume that
r>\frac{1}{2}
and
r+p<1
, this condition is indeed satisfied.

By using (
5.5
) and the fact that
0<2p<1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{|t|\leq 1}\hat{\theta}(t)2\Gamma(1-2p)\sin(\pi p)|t|^{2p-1}dt

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\xi\rVert^{2}2\Gamma(1-2p)\int_{|t|\leq 1}|t|^{2p-1}dt\leq C\Gamma(1-2p)p^{-1}\big\|\xi\big\|^{2}.

By using (
5.6
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{|t>1|}\hat{\theta}(t)2\Gamma(1-2p)\sin(\pi p)|t|^{2p-1}dt

\displaystyle\leq C_{\nu,r}\big\|\xi\big\|_{1+r}^{2}\Gamma(1-2p)\int_{|t|>1}(|t|+1)^{-2(1-r)}|t|^{2p-1}dt

\displaystyle\leq C_{\nu,r,1}\Gamma(1-2p)(1-r-p)\big\|\xi\big\|_{1+r}^{2}.

Then we finish the proof.

∎

Corollary 5.5
.

Assume conditions (
1
) and (
2
) in Proposition
5.2
are satisfied. Then for any
\xi\in W^{1+r}(\mathcal{H})
and any
0<p<1
such that
r+p<1
, the fractional equation

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega=\xi;

has a solution
\omega\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\omega\rVert\leq C_{\nu,r,p}\big\|\xi\big\|_{1+r}.

Proof.

explain uniform bound
∎

As the last step toward the proof of Proposition
5.2
, we prove the following result. In the proof, we introduce a new operator defined via a projection-valued measure. This operator was originally employed by R. Howe to study the decay of matrix coefficients and was later used by the author to construct smooth operators for applying the KAM method. In the present paper, the operator is applied in a distinct manner: it plays a key role in our analysis of the cohomological equation, yielding partial smooth estimates for the solution.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
we can define a linear operator
\mathcal{P}_{f}
on
\mathcal{H}
with the following properties:

(1)

for any
\xi,\,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle\xi,\mathcal{P}_{f}\eta\rangle;

(2)

for any
g_{1},\,g_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and any
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{g_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{g_{2}}=\mathcal{P}_{g_{1}g_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad c_{1}\mathcal{P}_{g_{1}}+c_{2}\mathcal{P}_{g_{2}}=\mathcal{P}_{c_{1}g_{1}+c_{2}g_{2}};

(3)

\lVert\mathcal{P}_{f}\rVert\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}
;

(4)

for any
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
we have

\displaystyle\xi=\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi+\mathcal{P}_{1-f}\xi;

(5)

if
f(x)=0
when
|x|\leq 1
, then for any
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
and any
s>0
, the equation
|U|^{s}\omega=\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi
has a solution
\omega\in\mathcal{H}
with estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\omega\rVert\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\xi\rVert;

(6)

if
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, set
f_{1}(x)=f^{\prime}(x)\cdot x
and
f_{2}(x)=f^{\prime\prime}(x)\cdot x\textrm{i}
. Then
we have

\displaystyle X^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{f}X^{2}-4\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}X+4\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}},

\displaystyle V^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{f}V^{2}+i\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(XV+VX)-2\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}}V-\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}}X^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}+2xf^{\prime\prime\prime}}X+\mathcal{P}_{(f_{2})_{2}},

\displaystyle U^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}}.

Proof.

We consider the restricted representation
\rho|_{G_{1}}
. Then we have a direct integral decomposition:

\displaystyle\rho|_{G_{1}}=\int_{Z}\rho_{z}d\varsigma(z)

of irreducible unitary representations
(\rho_{z},\mathcal{H}_{z})
of
P
for some measure space
(Z,\varsigma)
(see Section
5.1
). For any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
by using the Fourier model in Section
5.3
for each
\rho_{z}
, we define
\mathcal{P}_{f}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi=\int_{Z}\eta_{z}d\varsigma(z)

where
\eta_{z}(\lambda)=f(\lambda)\xi(\lambda)
.

Thus, to prove Lemma
5.6
, it suffices to prove that for each
z\in Z
, the results hold. (
1
) to (
4
) are clear from the definition.

(
5
): We define
\omega_{z}(\lambda)=\frac{f(\lambda)\xi_{z}(\lambda)}{|\lambda|^{s}}
. Since
f(\lambda)=0
when
|\lambda|\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\frac{f(\lambda)}{|\lambda|^{s}}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\omega_{z}\rVert\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\xi_{z}\rVert.

Moreover, it follows from (
5.2
) of Section
5.3
that

\displaystyle U\omega_{z}=\frac{f(\lambda)\xi_{z}(\lambda)}{|\lambda|^{s}}\cdot|\lambda|^{s}=f(\lambda)\xi(\lambda).

Let
\omega=\int_{Z}\omega_{z}d\varsigma(z)
. Then we get the result.

(
6
): By using (
5.2
) of Section
5.3
, for any
\psi_{z}\in W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{z})
we have

\displaystyle X\mathcal{P}_{f}(\psi_{z})

\displaystyle=X\big(f(\lambda)\psi_{z}(\lambda)\big)=f(\lambda)X\big(\psi_{z}(\lambda)\big)-2\lambda f^{\prime}(\lambda)\psi_{z}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{P}_{f}X-2\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}})(\psi_{z}).

\displaystyle U\mathcal{P}_{f}(\psi_{z})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{f}U(\psi_{z})=-i\lambda\cdot f(\lambda)\psi_{z}(\lambda)=-i\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}(\psi_{z}).

\displaystyle V\mathcal{P}_{f}(\psi_{z})

\displaystyle=i\left((\nu-1)\frac{d}{d\lambda}-\lambda\frac{d^{2}}{d\lambda^{2}}\right)\big(f(\lambda)\psi_{z}(\lambda)\big)

\displaystyle=if^{\prime}\cdot X\psi_{z}+f\cdot V\psi_{z}-i\lambda f^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)\,\psi_{z}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{P}_{f}V+i\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}X-\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}})(\psi_{z}).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle X\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{f}X-2\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}},

\displaystyle V\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{f}V+i\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}X-\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}},

\displaystyle U\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{f}U=-i\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}.

By keeping using the first relation we have

\displaystyle X^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=X(\mathcal{P}_{f}X-2\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}})=X\mathcal{P}_{f}X-2X\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{P}_{f}X-2\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}})X-2(\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}X-2\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{f}X^{2}-4\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}X+4\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}}.

From the second relation we have

\displaystyle V^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=V(\mathcal{P}_{f}V+i\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}X-\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}})=(V\mathcal{P}_{f})V+i(V\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}})X-V\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{P}_{f}V+i\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}X-\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}})V+i(\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}V+i\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}}X-\mathcal{P}_{(f^{\prime})_{2}})X

\displaystyle-(\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}}V+i\mathcal{P}_{(f_{2})^{\prime}}X-\mathcal{P}_{(f_{2})_{2}})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\mathcal{P}_{f}V^{2}+i\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(XV+VX)-2\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}}V-\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}}X^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}+2xf^{\prime\prime\prime}}X+\mathcal{P}_{(f_{2})_{2}}.

Here in
(1)
we use the fact that

\displaystyle-i\mathcal{P}_{(f^{\prime})_{2}}X-i\mathcal{P}_{(f_{2})^{\prime}}X=\mathcal{P}_{-i(f^{\prime})_{2}-i(f_{2})^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}+2xf^{\prime\prime\prime}}X.

From the third relation we have

\displaystyle U^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}

\displaystyle=U\bigl(-i\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}\bigr)=-i\,U\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}=-i\Bigl(-i\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}}\Bigr)=(-i)^{2}\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}}.

Then we finish the proof.
∎

5.4.1.
Proof of Proposition
5.2

Fix
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\displaystyle f(t)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }|t|\leq 1,\\
0,&\text{if }|t|\geq 2.\end{cases}

It follows from (
4
) of Lemma
5.6
that we have a decomposition

(5.7)

\displaystyle\xi=\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi+\mathcal{P}_{1-f}\xi.

We will solve the two equations

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi\quad\text{and}\quad|U|^{p}\omega_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{1-f}\xi

respectively, and then obtain the corresponding estimates.

It follows from (
5
) of Lemma
5.6
that
the equation

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{1-f}\xi

has a solution
\omega_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
with the Sobolev estimate

(5.8)

\displaystyle\|\omega_{2}\|\leq C_{f}\|\xi\|.

To apply Corollary
5.5
to study the equation

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi,

we need to obtain Sobolev estimates of
\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi
at first.

For any
\psi\in W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, (
3
) of Lemma
5.6
shows that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{P}_{f}\psi\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}\lVert\psi\rVert.

Moreover, (
6
) of Lemma
5.6
shows that

\displaystyle\|X^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}\psi\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{f}X^{2}\psi\|+4\|\mathcal{P}_{f_{1}}X\psi\|+4\|\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}}\psi\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{f}(\lVert X^{2}\psi\rVert+\lVert X\psi\rVert+\lVert\psi\rVert),

and

\displaystyle\lVert V^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}\psi\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{f}V^{2}\psi\|+\|\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(XV+VX)\psi\|+2\|\mathcal{P}_{f_{2}}V\psi\|+\|\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}}X^{2}\psi\|

\displaystyle+\|\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime\prime}+2xf^{\prime\prime\prime}}X\psi\|+\|\mathcal{P}_{(f_{2})_{2}}\psi\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{f}(\|V^{2}\psi\|+\|(XV+VX)\psi\|+2\|V\psi\|+\|X^{2}\psi\|

\displaystyle+\|X\psi\|+\|\psi\|),

and finally,

\displaystyle\|U^{2}\mathcal{P}_{f}\psi\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{(f_{1})_{1}}\psi\|\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{f}\|\psi\|.

Here in
(1)
we use (
3
) of Lemma
5.6
.

We note that
X
and
V
generate a subalgebra; and let
S_{1}
denote the corresponding subgroup. The above estimates shows that
\mathcal{P}_{f}\psi\in W^{2}(\mathcal{H})
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{P}_{f}\psi\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}\lVert\psi\rVert_{S_{1},2}\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{f,1}\big\|(I-V^{2}-X^{2})\psi\big\|.

Here in
(2)
we use the fact that
I-V^{2}-X^{2}
is the Laplacian for
S_{1}
.

This together with (
5.9
) and the interpolation theorem, we see that: for any
0<r<1
and any

\displaystyle\psi\in\mathcal{H}\text{ with }(I-V^{2}-X^{2})^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\psi\in\mathcal{H},

we have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{P}_{f}\psi\rVert_{1+r}

\displaystyle\leq C_{f,r}\big\|(I-V^{2}-X^{2})^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\psi\big\|.

This estimate allows us to apply Corollary
5.5
to obtain: for any
0<p<1
such that
r+p<1
, the fractional equation

\displaystyle|U|^{p}\omega=\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi

has a solution
\omega\in\mathcal{H}
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\omega\rVert\leq C_{\nu,r,p}\big\|\mathcal{P}_{f}\xi\big\|_{1+r}\leq C_{f,\nu,r,p}\big\|(I-V^{2}-X^{2})^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\xi\big\|.

This completes the proof.

5.5.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

Before we proceed to the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we list two lemmas that will be used for the proof.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is an essentially self-adjoint on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and satisfies

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle\geq\lVert\vartheta\rVert^{2}\qquad\forall\,\vartheta\in\text{Dom}(\mathcal{A}).

Then

(1)

if
\mathcal{A}^{r}\vartheta\in\mathcal{H}
for some
r>0
and
\vartheta\in\mathcal{H}
, then
\mathcal{A}^{a}\vartheta\in\mathcal{H}
for any
0\leq a\leq r
;

(2)

for any
r\leq 0
,
\mathcal{A}^{r}
is a bounded linear map on
\mathcal{H}
with
\lVert\mathcal{A}^{r}\rVert\leq 1
.

Proof.

We recall spectral theory in Section
5.2
. The assumption implies that the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
is contained in
[1,\infty)
. Then for any measurable function
f
defined on
[1,\infty)
, one can define the operator
f(\mathcal{A})
.

(
1
): We note that for any
0\leq a\leq r
, the function
x\mapsto x^{a}
is dominated by
x\mapsto x^{r}
on
[1,\infty)
.
Then it follows from the spectral calculus that
\mathcal{A}^{a}\vartheta
belongs to
\mathcal{H}
.

(
2
): If
r\leq 0
, then for every
x\geq 1
we have
x^{r}\leq 1
. Consequently, the operator
\mathcal{A}^{r}
is bounded with
\|\mathcal{A}^{r}\|\leq 1
.

∎

Lemma 5.8
.

We have: for any
1\leq i,j\leq n
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}}\Sigma_{j}^{c_{j}}=\Sigma_{j}^{c_{j}}\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}},\qquad\forall\,c_{i},\,c_{j}\in\mathbb{R}

and

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}}|U_{j}|^{r}=|U_{j}|^{r}\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}},\qquad\forall\,c_{i},\,r\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

We recall the direct integral decomposition in Section
5.1
. Because
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
commute, we see that

(\pi|_{S_{i}S_{j}},\,\mathcal{H})
decomposes into a direct integral of irreducible representations of the form:
(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2},\,\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})
, where
(\rho_{1},\mathcal{H}_{1})
is an irreducible representation of
S_{i}
and
(\rho_{2},\mathcal{H}_{2})
is an irreducible representation of
S_{j}
. It is clear that

\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}}\Sigma_{j}^{c_{j}}=\Sigma_{j}^{c_{j}}\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}}\quad\text{on }\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}.

Since
\mathcal{H}
decomposes into a direct sum of tensor products
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
, it follows that

\Sigma_{i}^{r_{i}}\Sigma_{j}^{r_{j}}=\Sigma_{j}^{r_{j}}\Sigma_{i}^{r_{i}}\quad\text{on }\mathcal{H}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}}|U_{j}|^{r}=|U_{j}|^{r}\Sigma_{i}^{c_{i}},\qquad\forall\,c_{i},\,r\in\mathbb{R}.

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

We prove the following result: for any
1\leq i\leq n
and any

\displaystyle\xi\in\mathcal{H}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}+1}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}\xi\in\mathcal{H},

the equation

\displaystyle|U_{1}|^{p_{1}}|U_{2}|^{p_{2}}\cdots|U_{i}|^{p_{i}}\omega_{i}=\xi

has a solution
\omega_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
with the estimates

\displaystyle\lVert\omega_{i}\rVert\leq C_{\tau_{i},\mathfrak{r}_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\big\|\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}\xi\big\|,

where
\tau_{i}=(\nu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{i})
,
\mathfrak{r}_{i}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{i})
and
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=(p_{1},\cdots,p_{i})
.

We prove this result by induction on
i
. Proposition
5.2
shows that the result holds for
n=1
. Suppose the result holds for some
i
. Now, suppose that
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
satisfying

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}+1}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}\xi\in\mathcal{H}.

Lemma
5.8
shows that

(5.10)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}+1}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}(\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\xi)=\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}+1}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}\xi\in\mathcal{H}.

Keeping using (
2
) of Lemma
5.7
, we see that the fact that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}+1}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}(\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\xi)\in\mathcal{H}

implies that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\xi\in\mathcal{H}.

From (
5.10
) and (
5.11
), it follows from the inductive assumption that the equation

(5.12)

\displaystyle|U_{1}|^{p_{1}}|U_{2}|^{p_{2}}\cdots|U_{i}|^{p_{i}}\psi=\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\xi

has a solution
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
with the estimate

(5.13)

\displaystyle\lVert\psi\rVert\leq C_{\tau_{i},\mathfrak{r}_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\big\|\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}(\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\xi)\big\|.

It follows from (
2
) of Lemma
5.7
that
\Sigma_{i+1}^{-\frac{r_{i}+1}{2}}
a bounded linear map on
\mathcal{H}
and

(5.14)

\displaystyle\theta=\Sigma_{i+1}^{-\frac{r_{i}+1}{2}}\psi\in\mathcal{H}.

From (
5.12
) we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i+1}^{-\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}

\displaystyle\big(|U_{1}|^{p_{1}}|U_{2}|^{p_{2}}\cdots|U_{i}|^{p_{i}}\psi\big)=\Sigma_{i+1}^{-\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}(\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\xi)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle|U_{1}|^{p_{1}}|U_{2}|^{p_{2}}\cdots|U_{i}|^{p_{i}}(\Sigma_{i+1}^{-\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\psi)=\xi

(5.15)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle|U_{1}|^{p_{1}}|U_{2}|^{p_{2}}\cdots|U_{i}|^{p_{i}}\theta=\xi.

Here in
(1)
we use Lemma
5.8
.

From (
5.14
) and (
5.13
), it follows from Proposition
5.2
that the equation

(5.16)

\displaystyle|U_{i+1}|^{p_{i+1}}\omega_{i+1}=\theta

has a solution
\omega_{i+1}\in\mathcal{H}
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\omega_{i+1}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{\nu_{i},r_{i+1},p_{i}}\big\|\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}}{2}}\theta\big\|=C_{\nu_{i},r_{i+1},p_{i}}\big\|\psi\big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{\tau_{i+1},\mathfrak{r}_{i+1},\mathfrak{p}_{i+1}}\big\|\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}(\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\xi)\big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}C_{\tau_{i+1},\mathfrak{r}_{i+1},\mathfrak{p}_{i+1}}\big\|\Sigma_{i+1}^{\frac{r_{i+1}+1}{2}}\Sigma_{i}^{\frac{r_{i}}{2}}\cdots\Sigma_{1}^{\frac{r_{1}+1}{2}}\xi\big\|

Here in
(1)
we use (
5.13
); in
(2)
we use (
5.10
).

It follows from (
5.5
) and (
5.16
) that

\displaystyle|U_{1}|^{p_{1}}|U_{2}|^{p_{2}}\cdots|U_{i}|^{p_{i}}|U_{i+1}|^{p_{i+1}}\omega_{i+1}=\xi.

This completes the induction step. By induction, the result holds for all
1\leq i\leq n
, which in particular implies Theorem
5.1
.

6.
The proof of semisimple case

Suppose
G
is a connected semisimple Lie group of the non-compact
type with finite center. Let
A
be a maximal split torus of
G
and
A^{+}
the closed positive Weyl chamber of
A
.
Denote by
\Phi
the set of non-multipliable roots of
A
and by
\Phi^{+}

the set of positive roots in
\Phi
.
The group
G
contains a connected
split subgroup
G_{0}
such that we have
A\subset G_{0}
and
\Phi=\Phi(A,G_{0})
.

We introduce some notations before we introduce the results.

6.1.
Notations and basic results

1
.

Let
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
be a unitary representation of
G

with a strong spectral gap, i.e.,
the restriction of
\rho
to each simple factor of
G
is isolated
from the trivial representation with respect to the Fell topology on the dual space.

In particular
\pi
Let

\displaystyle L^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{X})=\big\{f\in\mathcal{X}:\int_{\mathcal{X}}fdx=0\big\}.

2
.

We denote by
C^{s,H}(\mathcal{X})
the space of functions on
\mathcal{X}
that are continuous and have all derivatives (taken only along the directions tangent to the subgroup
H
, i.e., along elements of the Lie algebra of
H
) up to order
s
uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{X}
. For any
f\in C^{s,H}(\mathcal{X})
, define

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{H,C^{s}}=\sup\Bigl\{|\partial^{\beta}f(x)|:x\in\mathcal{X},\,\beta\text{ is a multi-index }

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{corresponding to derivatives along }H,\;|\beta|\leq s\Bigr\}.

We recall notation Sobolev space
W^{s,H}(\mathcal{H})
in (
1
) of Section
3.1
and Section
4
. It is clear that for any
\xi\in C^{s,H}(\mathcal{X})
, then
\xi\in W^{s,H}(L^{2}(\mathcal{X}))
(with respect to
\pi
) we have

(6.1)

\displaystyle\lVert\xi\rVert_{H,s}\leq\lVert\xi\rVert_{H,C^{s}}.

Note
. This part applies to general cases, even for nilmanifolds cases.

3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{S}=\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{l}\}
of
\Phi^{+}
is a strongly orthogonal system. For each
\alpha_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq l

choose non-trivial
U_{\alpha_{i}}
inside the one-dimensional root space of
\alpha_{i}
in
\text{Lie}(\mathbb{G}_{0})
. Then there exists

U_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}
inside the one-dimensional root space of
\alpha_{i}^{-1}
such that
\{U_{\alpha_{i}},U_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}},X_{\alpha_{i}}=[U_{\alpha_{i}},U_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}]\}
forms an
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
-triple. Let

(1)

x_{\alpha_{i}}
denote the one-dimensional subgroup generated by
X_{\alpha_{i}}
;

(2)

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{i}}
denote the subgroup generated by
x_{\alpha_{i}}
and
u_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}
in
\mathbb{G}_{0}
;

(3)

u_{\alpha_{i}}
denote the corresponding one-dimensional root subgroup generated by
U_{\alpha_{i}}
in
\mathbb{G}_{0}
;

(4)

H_{\alpha_{i}}
denote the closed subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{0}
generated by
u_{\pm\alpha_{i}}
. Then we have
\text{Lie}(H_{\alpha_{i}})=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
.

4
.

For each
\alpha_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq l
, let . Let Since
\rho
has strong spectral gap without fixed vectors, for any
1\leq i\leq l
,

\rho|_{H_{\alpha_{i}}}
has a spectral gap without fixed vectors, which we denote by
\mu_{i}
. Let

\displaystyle\nu_{i}:=\begin{cases}\sqrt{1-4\mu_{i}}\qquad&\text{if }\mu_{i}<\frac{1}{4},\\
0\qquad&\text{if }\mu_{i}\geq\frac{1}{4}.\end{cases}

5
.

Set

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}=(I-X_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}^{2}-U_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq l,

\displaystyle S(\mathcal{S})=\Pi_{1\leq i\leq l}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{i}},\quad u(\mathcal{S})=\Pi_{1\leq i\leq l}u_{\alpha_{i}},\quad H(\mathcal{S})=\Pi_{1\leq i\leq l}H_{\alpha_{i}}.

For any sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, set

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S})=\Pi_{1\leq i\leq l}(\Sigma_{i})^{\frac{1+\nu_{i}}{2}+\epsilon}.

For any
a\in A
, there exists
w
inside the Weyl group such that
a^{+}=w^{-1}aw\in A^{+}
. Define

\displaystyle\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},a)=\Pi_{1\leq i\leq l}\alpha_{i}(a^{+})^{\frac{-1+\nu_{i}}{2}+\epsilon}.

Note
. From the definition, it is clear that

\displaystyle\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},a)=\eta_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S},a^{+}),\qquad\forall\,a\in A.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a strongly orthogonal system of
A
and let

\displaystyle r_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq l}(\frac{1+\nu_{i}}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{r}(\mathcal{S})=\lfloor\sum_{1\leq i\leq l}(\frac{1+\nu_{i}}{2})\rfloor+1+l

\displaystyle r(\mathcal{S})=\lfloor\sum_{1\leq i\leq l}(\frac{1+\nu_{i}}{2})\rfloor+1.

6
.

We consider the subgroup
H(\mathcal{S})
(see (
5
)) and the restricted representation
\rho|_{H(\mathcal{S})}
. Let

\displaystyle r_{i}=\frac{1+\nu_{i}}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2},\qquad p_{i}=\frac{1-\nu_{i}}{2}-\frac{3\epsilon}{4},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l.

Then we have:

(1)

for each
1\leq i\leq l
,
\text{Lie}(H_{\alpha_{i}})=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
(see (
3
)). Moreover, since
\mathcal{S}
is a strongly orthogonal system,
\text{Lie}(H_{\alpha_{i}})
mutually commute. Hence,

\displaystyle\text{Lie}(H(\mathcal{S}))=\underbrace{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})}_{l};

(2)

for any
1\leq i\leq l
,

\rho|_{H_{\alpha_{i}}}
has a spectral gap without fixed vectors, and we denote the spectral gap by
\mu_{i}
(see (
4
) of Section
6.1
);

(3)

we have

\displaystyle\frac{1+\nu_{i}}{2}<r_{i}<1\quad\text{and}\quad r_{i}+p_{i}=1-\frac{\epsilon}{4},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l.

7
.

For any
v_{i}\in\text{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
,
1\leq i\leq m
and any
g\in\mathbb{G}
, we have

\displaystyle v_{1}\cdots v_{m}\pi(g)=(\Pi_{i=1}^{m}\|\text{Ad}_{g^{-1}}v_{i}\|)\cdot\pi(g)\tilde{v}_{1}\cdots\tilde{v}_{m},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{i}=\frac{\text{Ad}_{g^{-1}}v_{i}}{\|\text{Ad}_{g^{-1}}v_{i}\|},\qquad 1\leq i\leq m.

This factorization allows us to extract the norm factors
\|\text{Ad}_{g^{-1}}v_{i}\|
from the multiple partial derivatives. In particular, if

\displaystyle\|\text{Ad}_{g^{-1}}v_{i}\|\leq 1,\qquad 1\leq i\leq m,

then for any
\xi\in W^{m}(\mathcal{H})
we have

\displaystyle\|v_{1}\cdots v_{m}\pi(g)(\xi)\|\leq\|\pi(g)\tilde{v}_{1}\cdots\tilde{v}_{n}(\xi)\|.

8
.

For any
1\leq i\leq l
and any
a\in A
we have

\displaystyle\rho(a^{-1})\Sigma_{i}^{2}\rho(a)

\displaystyle=\rho(a^{-1})(I-X_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}^{2}-U_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}^{2})\rho(a)

\displaystyle=I-X_{\alpha_{i}^{-1}}^{2}-\alpha_{i}(a)^{2}U_{\alpha^{-1}_{i}}^{2}.

In particular, if
\alpha_{i}(a)<1
, then the term
\alpha_{i}(a^{n})^{2}
will not increases the contribution of the corresponding Sobolev derivative. More precisely, for any
\xi\in W^{\infty,S(\mathcal{S})}(\mathcal{H})
,

\displaystyle\big\|\Sigma_{i}^{2}(\rho(a)\xi)\big\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|\rho(a)\big\{\big(I-X_{\alpha_{i}}^{2}-\alpha_{i}(a)^{2}U_{\alpha^{-1}_{i}}^{2}\big)\xi\big\}\Big\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|\big(I-X_{\alpha_{i}}^{2}-\alpha_{i}(a)^{2}U_{\alpha^{-1}_{i}}^{2}\big)\xi\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\Sigma_{i}^{2}\xi\big\|.

9
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\subset G\ltimes_{\iota}V
be an abelian subgroup. Then there exists
v\in V
, an
\mathbb{R}
-split Cartan subgroup
A
of
G
and
a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\in A
such that the following hold:

•

for any
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,
v^{-1}zv
can be uniquely decomposed as

\displaystyle v^{-1}zv=s_{z}w_{z}

where
s_{z}\in A
and
w_{z}
is an element that commutes with
s_{z}
. Moreover, the map from
z\to s_{z}
is a group homomorphism; that is, for all
z_{1},\,z_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}
