\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

9.6.3.
Proof of (
1
) of Proposition
9.13

We can assume the notations and constructions in (
2
) of Section
9.1
, i.e., we can define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i,j}(d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}}),\quad\mathcal{F}_{i}(d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}}),\quad 1\leq i\leq l_{0},\,\,1\leq j\leq L(i)

for
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}}
correspondingly. Then

(9.32)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}=\mathcal{F}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{F}_{l_{0}}.

We recall that on each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
,
\chi_{i,1}<\cdots<\chi_{i,L(i)}
are the Lyapunov exponents of
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{i}=\mathcal{L}_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{L}_{i,L(i)}

be the corresponding Lyapunov subspace decomposition.

Lemma 9.14
.

We assume conditions in Proposition
9.13
. Then for any
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
and any
m\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}.

Proof.

Since

d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}}
is ergodic, for any
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
,
\chi_{i,1}\neq 0
. Since the Lyapunov subspace decomposition of
d\mathcal{A}
coincides with the decomposition (
9.30
), there is
\chi(i)\in\Lambda\backslash\{0\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i,1}=\mathcal{W}_{\chi(i)}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{k},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}.

It is harmless to assume that

\displaystyle\chi(i)(z_{1})<\chi(i)(z_{2})<\cdots<\chi(i)(z_{n}).

The assumption implies that

(9.33)

\displaystyle\chi(i)(z_{m}-z_{p})\leq-\mathfrak{r}\lVert z_{m}-z_{j}\rVert,\qquad\forall\,1\leq m<p\leq n.

We fix a basis
v_{i,1},\,v_{i,2},\cdots v_{i,\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}
of
\mathcal{L}_{i,1}
,
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
.

Step 1: We show that:

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{2})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

(9.34)

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ(z_{1}-z_{j}),f_{2}\circ(z_{2}-z_{j}),\cdots,f_{j-1}\circ(z_{j-1}-z_{j}),\mathcal{G}_{j}\big)\Big|,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{j}

\displaystyle=(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}\cdot\big((f_{j+1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\big)\cdot\big((f_{j+2})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+2}-z_{j})\big)

\displaystyle\quad\cdots\big((f_{n})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{n}-z_{j})\big).

In fact,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{2})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{j}\circ z_{j},(f_{j+1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{j+1},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{j},(f_{j+1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{j+1},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}\circ z_{j},\,(f_{j+1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{j+1},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ(z_{1}-z_{j}),\,f_{2}\circ(z_{2}-z_{j}),\cdots,f_{j-1}\circ(z_{j-1}-z_{j}),\mathcal{G}_{j}\big).

Here in
(1)
we recall that
f_{j}=(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}+(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}
(see (
1
) of Section
7.1
).

Then we get (
9.6.3
).

Step 2: We show that:
we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{j}=\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}g_{j,r},

where
g_{j,r}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
, such that for any
1\leq r\leq\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}
and any
m\in\mathbb{N}
the equation

\displaystyle v_{r}^{m}\varphi_{j,r,m}=g_{j,r}

has a solution
\varphi_{j,r,m}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
with the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\varphi_{j,r,m}\rVert\leq C_{m}\Pi_{p=j+1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}.

We will apply Corollary
9.6
to
\mathcal{G}_{j}
. To do so, we list the following observations:

(1)

\mathcal{L}_{i,1}
is a Diophantine subspace of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, which follows from (
2
) of Lemma
9.1
;

(2)

\mathcal{G}_{j}=(\mathcal{G}_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}
. Let

\displaystyle\xi_{j}

\displaystyle=\big((f_{j+1})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\big)\cdot\big((f_{j+2})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+2}-z_{j})\big)

\displaystyle\quad\cdots\big((f_{n})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{n}-z_{j})\big).

Then
\xi_{j}\in L_{\mathcal{F}_{i},o}
(see (
7.2
) of Section
7.1
). Then it follows from (
3
) of Lemma
7.2
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{j}=(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}\cdot\xi_{j}=(\xi_{j}\cdot f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}.

This gives that
\mathcal{G}_{j}=(\mathcal{G}_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}
.

(3)

We have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{G}_{j}\rVert_{\exp(\mathcal{F}_{i}),\,m\dim\mathfrak{n}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}\lVert(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}\rVert_{\exp(\mathcal{F}_{i}),\,C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}\lVert\xi_{j}\rVert_{\exp(\mathcal{F}_{i}),\,C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}(\Pi_{p=j+1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{0}})

\displaystyle\leq\Pi_{p=j}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}.

Here in
(1)
we recall (
6.1
) of Section
6.1
; in
(2)
we note that
\xi_{j}
is constant on
\mathcal{F}_{i}
and
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is
d\alpha
-invariant.

Then the statement follows from Corollary
9.6
.

Step 3: We show that:
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, any
1\leq r\leq\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}
and any
1\leq j\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{m,r,j}\big\|\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,ma_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m}},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{m,r,j}

\displaystyle=v_{r}^{m}\big(f_{1}\circ(z_{1}-z_{j})\cdot f_{2}\circ(z_{2}-z_{j})\cdots f_{j-1}\circ(z_{j-1}-z_{j})\big),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle a_{j}

\displaystyle=\min_{1\leq p\leq j-1}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert.

(
9.33
) implies that

\displaystyle\big\|d\alpha(z_{m}-z_{p})|_{\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\mathfrak{r}\lVert z_{m}-z_{p}\rVert}(1+\lVert z_{m}-z_{p}\rVert)^{\dim\mathfrak{n}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\lVert z_{m}-z_{p}\rVert}.

This together with (
4
) of Section
9.1
gives

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{m,r,j}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)ma_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m}}.

Then we complete the proof.

Conclusion:
For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any
1\leq j\leq n
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ(z_{1}-z_{j}),f_{2}\circ(z_{2}-z_{j}),\cdots,f_{j-1}\circ(z_{j-1}-z_{j}),\mathcal{G}_{j}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\Big|\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}\mathfrak{m}\big(\mathcal{Y}_{m,r,j},\,\varphi_{j,r,m}\big)\Big|\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{m,r,j}\big\|\cdot\lVert\varphi_{j,r,m}\rVert

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(3)}}{\leq}\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,ma_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m}}\cdot C_{m}\Pi_{p=j+1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m,1}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}.

Here in
(1)
we use Step
2
; in
(2)
we use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality; in
(3)
we use estimates in Steps
2
and
3
.

The above estimate and (
9.6.3
) complete the proof.
∎

Now we proceed to the proof of (
1
) of Proposition
9.13
. Set
\mathcal{K}_{[0]}=\{0\}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{[i]}=\mathcal{F}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{F}_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}.

Next, we define

\displaystyle f^{\{0\}}=f\quad\text{and}\quad f^{\{i\}}=f_{\mathcal{K}_{[i]},o},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}.

It is clear that

\displaystyle(f^{\{i\}})_{\mathcal{F}_{i+1},\,o}=f^{\{i+1\}},\qquad 0\leq i\leq l_{0}-1.

For any
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
, apply Lemma
9.14
with each
f_{j}
replaced
f_{j}^{\{i-1\}}
. This gives, for any
m\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}^{\{i-1\}}\circ z_{1},\,f_{2}^{\{i-1\}}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}^{\{i-1\}}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}^{\{i\}}\circ z_{1},\,f_{2}^{\{i\}}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}^{\{i\}}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}^{\{i-1\}}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}.

Summing these
l_{0}
inequalities telescopes the difference

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}^{\{l_{0}\}}\circ z_{1},f_{2}^{\{l_{0}\}}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}^{\{l_{0}\}}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|,

and since
f_{p}^{\{l_{0}\}}=f_{\mathcal{K}_{[l_{0}]},o}=(f_{p})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},o}
(see (
9.32
)), we have

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}.

This completes the proof.

9.6.4.
Proof of (
2
) of Proposition
9.13

Let
\mathcal{A}
be as described in (
3
) of Section
9.6.1
. Suppose
\mathfrak{n}_{k}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{k}
. We assume the notations and constructions in (
5
), (
6
) and (
7
) of Section
9.1
, i.e., we can define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i,j}(d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}),\quad\mathcal{F}_{i}(d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}),\quad 1\leq i\leq l_{0},\,\,1\leq j\leq L(i)

for
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
correspondingly.

We recall that on each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
,
\chi_{i,1}<\cdots<\chi_{i,L(i)}
are the Lyapunov exponents of
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{i}=\mathcal{L}_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{L}_{i,L(i)}

be the corresponding Lyapunov subspace decomposition.

Let
E_{i}=[\mathcal{F}_{i},\,\mathfrak{n}_{k-1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{n}_{k}
,
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
. Then

(9.35)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{k}=E_{1}+E_{2}\oplus\cdots+E_{l_{0}}.

Lemma 9.15
.

We assume conditions in Proposition
9.13
and suppose
\mathfrak{n}_{k}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{k}
. Then for any
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
and any
m\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{E_{i},o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{E_{i},o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{E_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}=\mathds{L}_{\chi_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathds{L}_{\chi_{l}}

be the corresponding Lyapunov subspace decomposition of
d\mathcal{A}
.

For each
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
,

(9.36)

\displaystyle V_{i,1}\;=\;\mathrm{span}\{X_{i,1}^{p}:1\leq p\leq\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}\}\subseteq\mathds{L}_{\chi_{i,1}}

is a lift of
\mathcal{L}_{i,1}
in
\mathfrak{n}
.

Since the Lyapunov subspace decomposition of
d\mathcal{A}
coincides with the decomposition (
9.30
), there is
\chi(i)\in\Lambda\backslash\{0\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathds{L}_{\chi_{i,1}}=\mathcal{W}_{\chi(i)},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}.

It is harmless to assume that

\displaystyle\chi(i)(z_{1})<\chi(i)(z_{2})<\cdots<\chi(i)(z_{n}).

The assumption implies that

(9.37)

\displaystyle\chi(i)(z_{m}-z_{p})\leq-\mathfrak{r}\lVert z_{m}-z_{j}\rVert,\qquad\forall\,1\leq m<p\leq n.

As in (
9.6.3
), we have

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{E_{i},o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{E_{i},o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{E_{i},o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

(9.38)

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ(z_{1}-z_{j}),f_{2}\circ(z_{2}-z_{j}),\cdots,f_{j-1}\circ(z_{j-1}-z_{j}),\mathcal{G}_{j}\big)\Big|,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{j}

\displaystyle=(f_{j})_{E_{i},\bot}\cdot\big((f_{j+1})_{E_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\big)\cdot\big((f_{j+2})_{E_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+2}-z_{j})\big)

\displaystyle\quad\cdots\big((f_{n})_{E_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{n}-z_{j})\big).

We will apply (
1
) of Proposition
9.7
to each
\mathcal{G}_{j}
.
To do so, we list the following observations:

(1)

V_{1}=\mathfrak{n}_{k-1}\cap\mathcal{W}_{-\chi(i)}
. We note that
V_{1}
is dual to
V_{i,1}
.
V_{2}=\mathrm{span}\{V_{1},\,E_{i}\}
.

(2)

Similar to (
2
) in the proof of Lemma
9.14
,
\mathcal{G}_{j}=(\mathcal{G}_{j})_{E_{[i]},\bot}
.

(3)

Let

\displaystyle\xi_{j}

\displaystyle=\big((f_{j+1})_{E_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\big)\cdot\big((f_{j+2})_{E_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{j+2}-z_{j})\big)

\displaystyle\quad\cdots\big((f_{n})_{E_{i},\,o}\circ(z_{n}-z_{j})\big).

We have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{G}_{j}\rVert_{\exp(V_{2}),\,\dim E_{i}+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}\lVert(f_{j})_{\mathcal{F}_{i},\bot}\rVert_{\exp(V_{2}),\,C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}\lVert\xi_{j}\rVert_{\exp(V_{2}),\,C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}(C_{\mathfrak{r}}\,\Pi_{p=j+1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim E_{i}+1}})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathfrak{r}}\,\Pi_{p=j}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}.

Here in
(1)
we recall (
6.1
) of Section
6.1
; in
(2)
we note that
\xi_{j}
is constant on
E_{i}
and
E_{i}
is
d\alpha
-invariant. This implies that

(9.39)

\displaystyle\lVert\xi_{j}\rVert_{\exp(E_{i}),\,C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}\leq\Pi_{p=j+1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{0}}.

From (
9.37
) we have

\displaystyle-\chi(i)(z_{p}-z_{j})\leq-\mathfrak{r}\lVert z_{m}-z_{j}\rVert,\qquad\forall\,1\leq j<p\leq n.

Then

\displaystyle\big\|d\alpha(z_{p}-z_{j})|_{V_{1}}\big\|\leq Ce^{-\mathfrak{r}\lVert z_{m}-z_{j}\rVert}(1+\dim\mathfrak{n})^{\lVert z_{m}-z_{j}\rVert}\leq C_{\mathfrak{r}}.

This together with (
4
) of Section
9.1
gives

(9.40)

\displaystyle\|\xi_{j}\|_{\exp(V_{1}),\,C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathfrak{r}}\,\Pi_{p=j+1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}.

Since
V_{2}=\mathrm{span}\{V_{1},\,E_{i}\}
, by Elliptic regularity theorem, it follows from (
9.39
) and (
9.40
) that

\displaystyle\|\xi_{j}\|_{\exp(V_{2}),\,C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathfrak{r}}\,\Pi_{p=j+1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}.

Then it follows from (
1
) of Proposition
9.7
that we can write

(9.41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{j}=\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}|X_{i,j}^{r}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}(\varphi_{r,j}),

where
\varphi_{r,j}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
with the estimates

\displaystyle\lVert\varphi_{r,j}\rVert\leq C_{\epsilon}\,\lVert\mathcal{G}_{j}\rVert_{\exp(V_{2}),\,\dim E_{i}+1}\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}\,\Pi_{p=j}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}.

Next, we show that for any
1\leq r\leq\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}
and any
1\leq j\leq n
, we have

(9.42)

\displaystyle\big\||X_{i,j}^{r}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}\eta_{j}\big\|\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{-\mathfrak{r}\,(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)a_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m}},

where

\displaystyle\eta_{j}

\displaystyle=f_{1}\circ(z_{1}-z_{j})\cdot f_{2}\circ(z_{2}-z_{j})\cdots f_{j-1}\circ(z_{j-1}-z_{j}),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle a_{j}

\displaystyle=\min_{1\leq p\leq j-1}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert.

In fact, by interpolation inequality we have

\displaystyle\big\||X_{i,j}^{r}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}\eta_{j}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}(\|\eta_{j}\|)^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(\big\||X_{i,j}^{r}|\eta_{j}\big\|)^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{\epsilon}\,(\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{0}})^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(\big\|X_{i,j}^{r}\eta_{j}\big\|)^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{\epsilon}\,(\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{0}})^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\big(C_{\epsilon}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)a_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,1}\,e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)a_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{1}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
2
) of Lemma
4.3
; in
(2)
(
9.37
) implies that (we recall notations in (
9.36
))

\displaystyle\big\|d\alpha(z_{m}-z_{p})|_{V_{i,1}}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\mathfrak{r}\lVert z_{m}-z_{p}\rVert}(1+\lVert z_{m}-z_{p}\rVert)^{\dim\mathfrak{n}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\lVert z_{m}-z_{p}\rVert}.

This together with (
4
) of Section
9.1
gives

(9.43)

\displaystyle\big\|X_{i,j}^{r}\eta_{j}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)a_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{1}}.

Conclusion:
For any
1\leq j\leq n
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ(z_{1}-z_{j}),f_{2}\circ(z_{2}-z_{j}),\cdots,f_{j-1}\circ(z_{j-1}-z_{j}),\mathcal{G}_{j}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\Big|\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}\mathfrak{m}\big(|X_{i,j}^{r}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}\eta_{j},\,\varphi_{j,r}\big)\Big|\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}\big\||X_{i,j}^{r}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}\eta_{j}\big\|\cdot\lVert\varphi_{j,r}\rVert

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(3)}}{\leq}\sum_{r=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,1}}C_{\epsilon,1}\,e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)a_{j}}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{j-1}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{1}}\cdot C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}\,\Pi_{p=j}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim E_{i}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r},1}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
9.41
) and (
1
) of Lemma
4.3
; in
(2)
we use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality; in
(3)
we use (
9.42
)
and (
9.43
).

The above estimate and (
9.6.4
) complete the proof.

∎

Now we proceed to the proof of (
2
) of Proposition
9.13
. Set
E_{[0]}=\{0\}
and

\displaystyle E_{[i]}=E_{1}+E_{2}+\cdots+E_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}-1.

Next, we define

\displaystyle f^{\{0\}}=f\quad\text{and}\quad f^{\{i\}}=f_{E_{[i]},o},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}.

It is clear that

\displaystyle(f^{\{i\}})_{E_{i+1},\,o}=f^{\{i+1\}},\qquad 0\leq i\leq l_{0}-1.

For any
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
, apply Lemma
9.15
with each
f_{j}
replaced
f_{j}^{\{i-1\}}
. This gives

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}^{\{i-1\}}\circ z_{1},\,f_{2}^{\{i-1\}}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}^{\{i-1\}}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}^{\{i\}}\circ z_{1},\,f_{2}^{\{i\}}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}^{\{i\}}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}^{\{i-1\}}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

Summing these
l_{0}
inequalities telescopes the difference

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}^{\{l_{0}\}}\circ z_{1},f_{2}^{\{l_{0}\}}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}^{\{l_{0}\}}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|,

and since
f_{p}^{\{l_{0}\}}=f_{E_{[l_{0}]},o}=(f_{p})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},o}
(see (
9.35
)), we have

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

This completes the proof.

9.6.5.
Proof of Theorem
9.12

Suppose

\displaystyle z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}\quad\text{ and }\quad f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}).

We prove by induction on the nilpotency step
k
of
N
.

Base Case
k=1
:
If
N
has step
1
, then
N\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{X}=N/\Gamma
is a torus. Then
d\mathcal{A}
is ergodic on
\mathfrak{n}
. In this case,
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}=\mathfrak{n}
and thus

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)=\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n}).

Then it follows from (
1
) of Proposition
9.13
that

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}.

Thus the result holds when
k=1
.

Inductive Step:
Suppose the result holds for all simply-connected nilpotent groups of step
\leq k-1
, and now let
N
have step
k\geq 2
. We have a decomposition of
d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{k}=\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)},

where
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(i)}=\mathfrak{n}_{k}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{0},i)}
,
i=1,\,2
.

We recall that
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{k}
(see Lemma
9.10
and (
3
) of Section
9.6.1
). Then
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}
is
\Gamma
-rational is and is
d\alpha
-invariant.

Lemma 9.16
.

The result holds for all simply-connected nilpotent groups of step
k
in either of the following two cases:

(1)

\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}=0

(2)

\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}=0
.

Proof.

Case I:
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}=0
.
Then
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}=\mathfrak{n}_{k}
. It follows from (
1
) of Proposition
9.13
that

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

(9.44)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}},

for any
m\geq 0
.

Case II:
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}=0
.
Then
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}=\mathfrak{n}_{k}
. It follows from (
2
) of Proposition
9.13
that

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

For either case, we have proved that

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

Let
E=\mathfrak{n}_{k}
. It follows from (
6
) of Lemma
7.2
that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{E,\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{E,\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{E,\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)

(9.45)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{1})_{E,o}\circ(\alpha(z_{1})_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})\cdot(f_{2})_{E,\,o}(\alpha(z_{2})_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})\cdots(f_{n})_{E,o}\circ(\alpha(z_{n})_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})d\varrho_{1,E}.

We recall that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{E}:=(N/\exp(E))\bigl/\bigl(\Gamma/(\exp(E)\cap\Gamma)\bigr)

(see (
2
) of Section
7
). It is clear that
N/\exp(E)=N/\exp(\mathfrak{n}_{k})
is simply connected of step
k-1
.

We can define
\Theta(\mathfrak{n}/E,\,d(\alpha_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}),\mathfrak{z})
correspondingly. We also have the Lyapunov subspace decomposition for
d\alpha|_{\mathfrak{n}/E}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}/E=\oplus_{\chi\in\Lambda_{1}}\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{\chi}

where
\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{\chi}=\mathcal{W}_{\chi}+E
. It is clear that
\Lambda_{1}\subseteq\Lambda
(see (
9.30
) of Section
9.6.1
). This implies that

\displaystyle\Theta(\mathfrak{n}/E,\,d\alpha_{\mathcal{X}_{E}},\mathfrak{z})\geq\Theta(\mathfrak{n},\,d\alpha,\mathfrak{z})\geq\mathcal{}\mathfrak{r}.

It follows from inductive assumption that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{1})_{E,o}\circ(\alpha(z_{1})_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})\cdots(f_{n})_{E,o}\circ(\alpha(z_{n})_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})d\varrho_{1,E}

\displaystyle-\big(\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{1})_{E,o}d\varrho_{1,E}\big)\cdots\big(\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{n})_{E,o}d\varrho_{1,E}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert(f_{p})_{E,o}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert(f_{p})_{E,o}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

(9.46)

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

Further, from (
7
) of Lemma
7.2
we have

(9.47)

\displaystyle\big(\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{1})_{E,o}d\varrho_{1,E}\big)\cdots\big(\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{n})_{E,o}d\varrho_{1,E}\big)=\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n}).

Consequently, it follows from (
9.6.5
), (
9.6.5
) and (
9.47
) that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{E,\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{E,\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{E,\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

This together with (
9.6.5
) give that: for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

Then we complete the proof.

∎

We now complete the inductive step. By Lemma
9.16
, it remains to consider the case of
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}\neq 0
and
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}\neq 0
.

It follows from (
1
) of Proposition
9.13
that

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{m}\big((f_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{1},(f_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{2},\cdots,(f_{n})_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)},\,o}\circ z_{n}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

(9.48)

\displaystyle+C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

for any
m\geq 0
.

Let
E=\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}
. We point out that (
9.6.5
) still holds for
E=\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}
. Let
N_{1}=N/\exp(E)
.
Then
N_{1}
is simply connected. Since
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}\neq 0
,
N_{1}
remains a
k
-step nilpotent group. We denote by
\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{\prime}
the
k
-th element of the descending central series of
\text{Lie}(N_{1})=\mathfrak{n}/\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}
. Then
d(\alpha|_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to
d\alpha|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}}
. Since
d\alpha|_{\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(2)}}
only has roots of unity,
(
2
) of Lemma
9.16
applies
and gives

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{1})_{E,o}\circ(\alpha(z_{1})_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})\cdots(f_{n})_{E,o}\circ(\alpha(z_{n})_{\mathcal{X}_{E}})d\varrho_{1,E}

\displaystyle-\big(\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{1})_{E,o}d\varrho_{1,E}\big)\cdots\big(\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}(f_{n})_{E,o}d\varrho_{1,E}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert(f_{p})_{E,o}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert(f_{p})_{E,o}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

(9.49)

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}

for any
m\geq 0
.

We note that (
9.47
) holds as well for
E=\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{(1)}
. It follows from (
9.6.5
), (
9.6.5
), (
9.6.5
) and (
9.47
) that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{m}\big(f_{1}\circ z_{1},f_{2}\circ z_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\circ z_{n}\big)-\mathfrak{m}(f_{1})\cdots\mathfrak{m}(f_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,m}e^{-(\mathfrak{r}-\epsilon)\,m\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{m\dim\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle+\delta C_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{r}}e^{-\mathfrak{r}(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon)\,\min_{1\leq p\neq j\leq n}\lVert z_{p}-z_{j}\rVert}\,\Pi_{p=1}^{n}\lVert f_{p}\rVert_{C^{\dim\mathfrak{n}+1}}.

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. This completes the inductive step. Hence, we complete the proof.

9.6.6.
Proof of Theorem
2.4

For any

\mathfrak{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}\setminus\{0\}
, where
z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
,
1\leq i\leq n
define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{z})=\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert}\in\mathbb{S}^{nl-1}.

For any
\chi\in\Lambda\backslash\{0\}
, define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\ker\chi}=\{\mathfrak{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\,\text{there exist }i,\,j\text{ such that }z_{i}-z_{j}\in\ker\chi\}.

Let

\mathcal{M}\;=\;\bigcup_{\chi\in\Lambda\setminus\{0\}}\widetilde{\ker\chi}.

Set

\mathcal{R}_{n}\;=\;\bigl\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\,\mathrm{dist}(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{z}),\,\mathcal{M})>0\bigr\}

and for each
\varepsilon>0
set

\mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon}\;=\;\bigl\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\,\mathrm{dist}(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{z}),\,\mathcal{M})\geq\varepsilon\bigr\}.

Lemma 9.17
.

For any
n\geq 2
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\delta>0
such that

(9.50)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}\;=1

and

(9.51)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\delta}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}\;\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Proof.

We equip
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with its standard Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
and denote by
\sigma
the surface measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
be any Borel set, and consider the cone

R_{U}\;=\;\{\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{nl}:\|x\|\leq 1,\;\frac{x}{\|x\|}\in U\}.

Then for any
r>0
, we have

\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r,\;\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert}\in U\}\;=\;\mathbb{Z}^{nl}\;\cap\;(r\cdot R_{U}).

We note that it applies because the boundary of
R_{U}
has measure zero. By the classical lattice point theorem (see
[
2
, Theorem 21]
)

\#\bigl(\mathbb{Z}^{nl}\cap(r\,R_{U})\bigr)\;=\;\Vol(r\,R_{U})\;+\;o\bigl(\Vol(r\,R_{U})\bigr)\;=\;r^{nl}\,\Vol(R_{U})\;+\;o(r^{nl}).

On the other hand, in polar coordinates

\displaystyle\Vol(R_{U})\;=\;\int_{0}^{1}r^{d-1}\,dr\;\int_{U}d\sigma(\omega)\;=\;\frac{1}{d}\,\sigma(U)

and

\Vol(B_{r})\;=\;r^{nl}\,\Vol(B_{1})\;=\;\frac{r^{nl}}{nl}\,\sigma\bigl(\mathbb{S}^{nl-1}\bigr).

Hence

\displaystyle\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r,\;\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert}\in U\}=r^{nl}\,\frac{\sigma(U)}{nl}\;+\;o(r^{nl})

\displaystyle=\Vol(B_{r})\,\frac{\sigma(U)}{\sigma(\mathbb{S}^{nl-1})}\;+\;o(r^{nl}).

By taking
U=\mathbb{S}^{nl-1}
, we have

\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}\;=\;\Vol(B_{r})+o(r^{nl}).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r,\;\frac{\mathfrak{z}}{\lVert\mathfrak{z}\rVert}\in U\}}{\#\{\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nl}:\|\mathfrak{z}\|\leq r\}}=\frac{\sigma(U)}{\sigma(\mathbb{S}^{nl-1})}.

Let
U=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
. observe that
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})
is a finite union of great subspheres in
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, hence

\sigma\bigl(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})\bigr)=0
. Then
\sigma(U)=1
. Thus, we get (
9.50
).

Let
U=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n,\delta})
. Choose
\delta>0
such that
\sigma(U)\geq 1-\varepsilon
. Thus, we get (
9.51
).

Since
\sigma\bigl(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{R}_{n})\bigr)=1
, the first statement follows by taking

U=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
. For the second, choose
\delta>0
so that

\sigma\bigl\{\omega:\mathrm{dist}(\omega,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M}))\geq\delta\bigr\}\;\geq\;1-\varepsilon,

and set
U=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n,\delta})
. The same counting argument gives the desired lower bound.
∎

Appendix A
Unitary representations of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})

Section
5.3
already discussed the direct integral decomposition for unitary representations of general type
I
Lie groups, but we can say more in the special case of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
.

Recall that
\sl(2,\mathbb{R})
is generated by the vector fields

\displaystyle X=\begin{pmatrix}{1}&0\\
0&{-1}\end{pmatrix},\quad U=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad V=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

The
Casimir
operator is given by

\displaystyle\Box:=-X^{2}-2(UV+VU)

and generates the center of the enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Any unitary representation
(\pi,\mathcal{H})
of
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
is decomposed into a direct integral (see
[
8
]
and
[
14
]
)

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\int_{\oplus}\mathcal{K}_{\mu}ds(\mu)

with respect to a positive Stieltjes measure
ds(\mu)
over the spectrum
\sigma(\Box)
. The
Casimir operator acts as the constant
\mu\in\sigma(\Box)
on every Hilbert representation space
\mathcal{K}_{\mu}
, which does not need to be irreducible.
In fact,
\mathcal{K}_{\mu}
is in general
the direct sum of an (at most countable) number of unitarily equivalent representation spaces
H_{\mu}
equal to the spectral multiplicity of
\mu\in\sigma(\Box)
. We say that
\pi
has a spectral gap
if there is some
u_{0}>0
such that
s((0,u_{0}])=0
. It is clear that if
\pi
has a spectral gap then
\pi
contains no non-trivial
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-fixed vectors.

The representation spaces
H_{\mu}
have unitarily equivalent models, which we also write as
H_{\mu}
, and each is one of the following four classes:

•

If
0<\mu<1
, then
H_{\mu}
is in the complementary
series.

•

If
\mu>1
, then
H_{\mu}
is in the principal series.

•

If
\mu=1
, then
H_{\mu}
is in the mock discrete series or
the principal series.

•

Otherwise if
\mu\leq 0
, then
H_{\mu}
is in the discrete series.

It will be convenient to describe these representations via models that use a representation parameter
\nu
satisfying

\mu=1-\nu^{2}\,,

where it is sufficient to take
\nu=\sqrt{1-\mu}
.
Below are standard models from the literature.

The line model of the principal or complementary series consists of functions on
\mathbb{R}

and has the following norm. For
\mu\geq 1
,
\|f\|_{H_{\mu}}:=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
,
and when
\mu>1
,

\|f\|_{H_{\mu}}:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{f(x)\overline{f(y)}}{|x-y|^{1-\nu}}dxdy\right)^{1/2}\,.

The group action on
H_{\mu}
is given by

A\cdot f(x):=|-bx+d|^{-(\nu+1)}f(\frac{ax-c}{-bx+d})\,,

where
x\in\mathbb{R}
and

(A.2)

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\SL(2,\mathbb{R})\,.

This yields the vector fields

\begin{array}[]{cc}V=-\frac{d}{dx}\,,\quad\quad U=(1+\nu)x+x^{2}\frac{d}{dx}\,,\quad\quad X=(1+\nu)+2x\frac{d}{dx}\,.\end{array}

The holomorphic discrete or mock discrete series consists of holomorphic functions on the upper half-plane,
\mathbb{H}
. Its counterpart, the anti-holomorphic discrete or mock discrete series, does not need to be considered because of a complex anti-linear isomorphism between the two spaces.
The holomorphic discrete or mock discrete series norm is given by

\|f\|_{H_{\mu}}=\begin{cases}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}}|f(x+iy)|^{2}\,y^{\nu-1}\,dx\,dy\right)^{1/2}&\quad\quad\nu\geq 1\,,\\
\left(\sup_{y>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x+iy)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}&\quad\quad\nu=0\,.\end{cases}

The group action is analogous to the one for the line model.
For
z\in\mathbb{H}
and
A
given by (
A.2
),
we have

A\cdot f(z):=|-bz+d|^{-(\nu+1)}f(\frac{az-c}{-bz+d}),\,,

which yields the vector fields

\begin{array}[]{ll}V=-\frac{d}{dz}\,,\quad\quad U=(1+\nu)z+z^{2}\frac{d}{dz}\,,\quad\quad X=(1+\nu)+2z\frac{d}{dz}\,.\end{array}

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma (
6.1
)

(
1
): Suppose
\xi,\,\eta
have the integral decompositions:

\displaystyle\xi=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\xi_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi)\quad\text{and}\quad\eta=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\eta_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi).

Then we have

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{T}\xi,\eta\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\big\langle|\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}\xi_{\chi},\,\eta_{\chi}\big\rangle d\mu(\chi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\big\langle\xi_{\chi},\,(\overline{|\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}}\eta_{\chi}\big\rangle d\mu(\chi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\big\langle\xi_{\chi},\,|\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}\eta_{\chi}\big\rangle d\mu(\chi)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\xi,|\mathfrak{u}_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\mathfrak{u}_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\mathfrak{u}_{m}|^{t_{m}}\eta\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\xi,\mathcal{T}\eta\rangle.

(
2
): We note that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{t_{1}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{t_{2}}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{m}^{t_{m}}\xi\big\|=\Big\|\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}(\chi_{1}\mathrm{i})^{t_{1}}\cdots(\chi_{m}\mathrm{i})^{t_{m}}\xi_{\chi}d\mu(\chi)\Big\|

\displaystyle=\big(\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}|\chi_{1}|^{2t_{1}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{2t_{m}}\lVert\xi_{\chi}\rVert^{2}d\mu(\chi)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\big\||\mathfrak{u}_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\mathfrak{u}_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\mathfrak{u}_{m}|^{t_{m}}\xi\big\|.

(
3
): We recall that for any
r\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\displaystyle\exp(r)=\exp(r_{1}\mathfrak{u}_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}\mathfrak{u}_{m})\quad\text{ and }\quad\pi(r)=\pi(\exp(r)).

We use
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
to denote the Schwartz space of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The representation
\pi|_{S_{1}}
extends to a
∗
-representation on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
: for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
,
\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)
is the operator on
\mathcal{H}
for which

(B.1)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)(\xi):=\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\hat{f}(r)\pi(r)(\xi)dr.

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
. For any
\xi,\,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)(\xi),\eta\bigl\rangle=\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\hat{f}(r)\langle\pi(r)\xi,\eta\rangle dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\hat{f}(r)\chi(r)\langle\xi_{\chi},\,\eta_{\chi}\rangle d\mu(\chi)dr

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\langle\xi_{\chi},\,\eta_{\chi}\rangle\Big(\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\hat{f}(r)\chi(r)dr\Big)d\mu(\chi)

(B.2)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}f(\chi)\langle\xi_{\chi},\,\eta_{\chi}\rangle d\mu(\chi).

Since

\displaystyle\lVert\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)\rVert\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\infty},\qquad\forall\,f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})

we can extend
\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)
from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
by taking strong limits of operators and pointwise monotone increasing limits of non-negative functions (see
[
11
]
for a detailed treatment). Hence
\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)
is a homomorphism of
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
to bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}
. In particular, for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
we see that

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)(\xi)=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}f(\chi)\xi_{\chi}d\mu(\chi),\qquad\forall\,\xi\in\mathcal{H}.

Then for any
h\in H
, any
\xi,\,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
and any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)(\pi(h)\xi),\pi(h)\eta\bigl\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\hat{f}(r)\big\langle\pi\big(\exp(r_{1}\mathfrak{u}_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}\mathfrak{u}_{m})\big)\pi(h)\xi,\,\pi(h)\eta\big\rangle dr

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\hat{f}(r)\big\langle\pi\big(\exp(r_{1}a_{1}\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{i}+\cdots+r_{m}a_{m}\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{m}\big)\xi,\eta\big\rangle dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}a_{1}^{-1}\cdots a_{m}^{-1}\hat{f}(\frac{r_{1}}{a_{1}},\cdots,\frac{r_{m}}{a_{m}})\big\langle\pi\big(\exp(r_{1}\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{i}+\cdots+r_{m}\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{m}\big)\xi,\eta\big\rangle dr

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}\bigl\langle\pi_{\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}}\big(f(a_{1}x_{1},\cdots,a_{m}x_{m})\big)(\xi),\,\pi(h)\eta\bigl\rangle.

Here in
(1)
we recall

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{\|\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}\|},\quad\text{and}\quad a_{i}=\|\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}\|,\quad 1\leq i\leq m;

in
(2)
we use (
B.1
) and denote by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}=\{\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{m}\}.

The above analysis shows that

(B.3)

\displaystyle\pi(h^{-1})\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f)\pi(h)=\pi_{\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}}\big(f(a_{1}x_{1},\cdots,a_{m}x_{m})\big)

for all
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
. By the extension argument above, the same identity holds for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
.

By spectral theory, there exists a regular Borel measure
\mu^{\prime}
on
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
such that every vector
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
can be decomposed as

\xi=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\xi^{\prime}_{\chi}\,d\mu^{\prime}(\chi),

and for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\pi\big(\exp(r_{1}\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{m})\big)\xi=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\chi(r)\,\xi^{\prime}_{\chi}\,d\mu^{\prime}(\chi).

For any
n>0
set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{n}=[-n,n]^{m}\subset\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}.

Let
1_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}
be the characteristic function of
\mathfrak{X}
and set

\displaystyle f_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}(x)=1_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}(x)|x_{1}|^{t_{1}}|x_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|x_{m}|^{t_{m}}.

If we decompose
\pi(h)\xi
as:

\displaystyle\pi(h)\xi=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}(\pi(h)\xi)_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi),

then we have

\displaystyle\pi(h^{-1})\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}1_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}(\chi)|\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\chi_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}(\pi(h)\xi)_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi)

\displaystyle=\pi(h^{-1})\pi_{\mathfrak{u}}(f_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}})\pi(h)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\pi_{\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}}\big(f_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}(a_{1}x_{1},\cdots,a_{m}x_{m})\big)

(B.4)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}1_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}(a_{1}\chi_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\chi_{m})|a_{1}\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}|a_{2}\chi_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|a_{m}\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}\xi^{\prime}_{\chi}\,d\mu^{\prime}(\chi)

Here in
(1)
we use (
B.3
).

It follows that

\displaystyle\pi(h^{-1})\mathcal{T}\pi(h)\xi

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\pi(h^{-1})\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}|\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\chi_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}(\pi(h)\xi)_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi(h^{-1})\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}1_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}(\chi)|\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\chi_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}(\pi(h)\xi)_{\chi}\,d\mu(\chi)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}1_{\mathfrak{X}_{n}}(a_{1}\chi_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\chi_{m})|a_{1}\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}|a_{2}\chi_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|a_{m}\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}\xi^{\prime}_{\chi}\,d\mu^{\prime}(\chi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}|a_{1}\chi_{1}|^{t_{1}}|a_{2}\chi_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|a_{m}\chi_{m}|^{t_{m}}\xi^{\prime}_{\chi}\,d\mu^{\prime}(\chi)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(3)}}{=}a_{1}^{t_{1}}\cdots a_{m}^{t_{m}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}|^{t_{1}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{2}|^{t_{2}}\cdots|\tilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{m}|^{t_{m}}\xi.

Here in
(1)
we use the fact that
\pi(h)\xi\in W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, which follows from the fact that
\xi\in W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
; in
(2)
we use (
B
);
in
(3)
we recall that

\xi\in W^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
.

Then we complete the proof.

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma (
6.1
)

Let
\mathcal{T}_{i}
stands for
\Sigma_{i}^{2}
or
|U_{i}|
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
l
. Suppose
l=1
. Then by interpolation inequality for any
x\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x}\xi\|\leq C_{x}\|\xi\|^{1-x}\cdot\|\mathcal{T}_{1}\xi\|^{x}.

This corresponds to the desired form with
c(0)=1-x
and
c(1)=x
.

Suppose the result holds for
l
. For any
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{l+1})\in[0,1]^{l+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x_{1}}

\displaystyle\cdots\mathcal{T}_{l+1}^{x_{l+1}}\xi\|\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\big\|\mathcal{T}_{l+1}^{x_{l+1}}(\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{l}^{x_{l}}\xi)\big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{x_{l+1}}\|\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{l}^{x_{l}}\xi\|^{1-x_{l+1}}\cdot\big\|\mathcal{T}_{l+1}^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{l}^{x_{l}}\xi)\big\|^{x_{l+1}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}C_{x_{l+1}}\|\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{l}^{x_{l}}\xi\|^{1-x_{l+1}}\cdot\big\|\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{l}^{x_{l}}(\mathcal{T}_{l+1}\xi)\big\|^{x_{l+1}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{x_{l+1}}\cdot\big(C_{\bar{x}}\prod_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}\big\|\mathcal{T}^{b}\xi\big\|^{c(b,\bar{x})}\big)^{1-x_{l+1}}

(C.1)

\displaystyle\cdot\big(C_{\bar{x}}\prod_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}\big\|\mathcal{T}^{b}(\mathcal{T}_{l+1}\xi)\big\|^{c(b,\bar{x})}\big)^{x_{l+1}}.

Here in
(1)
we note that the operators
\mathcal{T}_{i}
commute; in
(2)
we use the induction assumption on
l
(applied both to
\xi
and to
\mathcal{T}_{l+1}\xi
) and set
\bar{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{l})
. Further, the induction assumption gives

(C.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}c(b,\bar{x})b=\bar{x}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}c(b,\bar{x})=1.

Then for any
b=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{l+1})\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1}
, set

(C.3)

\displaystyle c(b,x)=\begin{cases}c(\bar{b},\bar{x})(1-x_{l+1}),&\text{if }b_{l+1}=0,\\
c(\bar{b},\bar{x})x_{l+1},&\text{if }b_{l+1}=1,\end{cases}

where
\bar{b}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{l})
.

Then it follows from (
C
) and (
C.3
) that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{l+1}^{x_{l+1}}\xi\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{x_{l+1}}C_{\bar{x}}^{1-x_{l+1}}C_{\bar{x}}^{x_{l+1}}\prod_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1}}\big\|\mathcal{T}^{b}\xi\big\|^{c(b,x)}

\displaystyle=C_{x}\prod_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1}}\big\|\mathcal{T}^{b}\xi\big\|^{c(b,x)}.

It remains to check that the new constants
c(b,x)
satisfy

\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1}}c(b,x)=1\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1}}c(b,x)b=x.

We note that

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1}}

\displaystyle c(b,x)b=\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1},b_{l+1}=0}c(b,x)b+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1},b_{l+1}=1}c(b,x)b

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}c(b,\bar{x})(1-x_{l+1})b+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1},b_{l+1}=1}c(\bar{b},\bar{x})x_{l+1}b

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}(1-x_{l+1})\bar{x}+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}c(\bar{b},\bar{x})x_{l+1}b+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}(\underbrace{0,\cdots,0}_{l},c(\bar{b},\bar{x})x_{l+1})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}(1-x_{l+1})\bar{x}+x_{l+1}\bar{x}+(\underbrace{0,\cdots,0}_{l},x_{l+1})

\displaystyle=x

and

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1}}c(b,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1},b_{l+1}=0}c(b,x)+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l+1},b_{l+1}=1}c(b,x)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}c(b,\bar{x})(1-x_{l+1})+\sum_{b\in\mathbb{L}_{l}}c(b,\bar{x})x_{l+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}1(1-x_{l+1})+1\cdot x_{l+1}

\displaystyle=1.

Here in
(1)
we use (
C.3
), in
(2)
we use (
C.2
).

Thus, we proved the case of
l+1
. Then we finish the proof.

Appendix D
Proof of Lemma
6.2

Define

I=\{i\in\{1,\dots,k\}:b_{i}=1\}.

We have

\displaystyle U^{b}\big(\Pi_{i=1}^{l}\pi(a)^{t_{i}}(f_{i}\circ s_{i})\big)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\sqcup I_{2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m}=I\\
I_{i}\cap I_{j}=\varnothing\,(i\neq j)\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\partial_{I_{i}}\big(\pi(a^{n_{i}})f_{i}\circ s_{i}\big),

where

\partial_{I_{i}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{j\in I_{i}}U_{\alpha_{j}},&\text{if }I_{i}\neq\varnothing,\\[2.84526pt]
I,&\text{if }I_{i}=\varnothing.\end{cases}

For each
i
and sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, by using (
7
) of Section
6.1
we have

\displaystyle\big\|\partial_{I_{i}}

\displaystyle\big(\pi(a^{t_{i}})f_{i}\circ s_{i}\big)\big\|_{C^{0}}\leq C\,\big(\Pi_{j\in I_{i}}\alpha_{j}(a^{-t_{i}})\big)\lVert f_{i}\circ s_{i}\rVert_{C^{|I_{i}|}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathcal{Z}}(|t_{i}|+1)^{\dim(G\ltimes_{\iota}V)|I_{i}|}\big(\Pi_{j\in I_{i}}\alpha_{j}(a^{-t_{i}})\big)\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{|I_{i}|}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{\mathcal{Z},\varepsilon,m}\big(\Pi_{j\in I_{i}}\alpha_{j}(a^{-t_{i}(1-\varepsilon)})\big)\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{|I_{i}|}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathcal{Z},\varepsilon,m}\big(\Pi_{j\in I_{i}}\alpha_{j}(a^{-t(1-\varepsilon)})\big)\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{|I_{i}|}}.

Here in
(1)
we used the assumption that
\alpha_{j}(a^{-t_{i}})<1
for any
j
and
|I_{i}|\leq m
.

Then we have

\displaystyle\big\|U^{b}\big(\Pi_{i=1}^{l}\pi(a)^{t_{i}}(f_{i}\circ s_{i})\big)\big\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\sqcup I_{2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m}=I\\
I_{i}\cap I_{j}=\varnothing\,(i\neq j)\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}C_{\mathcal{Z},\varepsilon,m}\big(\Pi_{j\in I_{i}}\alpha_{j}(a^{-t(1-\varepsilon)})\big)\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{|I_{i}|}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{\mathcal{Z},\varepsilon,m,1}\big(\alpha(a^{-t(1-\varepsilon)})\big)^{b}\Pi_{i=1}^{m}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{|b|}}.

Here in
(1)
we use the fact that

\Bigl(\alpha\bigl(a^{-t(1-\varepsilon)}\bigr)\Bigr)^{b}=\prod_{j=1}^{l}\alpha_{j}\bigl(a^{-t(1-\varepsilon)}\bigr)^{b_{j}}=\prod_{j\in I}\alpha_{j}\bigl(a^{-t(1-\varepsilon)}\bigr).

Then we complete the proof.

Appendix E
Proof of Lemma (
7.2
)

We assume notations in the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

(
1
): We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}g\cdot f_{E,o}d\varrho

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\langle f_{E,o},\,\bar{g}\rangle=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{\dim E}}\langle(f_{E,o})_{z},\,(\bar{g})_{z}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{z}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}\big\langle(f_{E,o})_{0},\,(\bar{g})_{0}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}=\langle f_{E,o},\,(\overline{g})_{E,o}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle f_{E,o},\,\overline{g_{E,o}}\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{E,o}\cdot g_{E,o}d\varrho.

Here in
(1)

\bar{g}
is the complex conjugate of
g
; in
(2)
we use (
7.9
).

(
2
) is a direct consequence of (
7.9
) and the orthogonality between the nonzero Fourier components
g_{E,\bot}

with the zero Fourier component
f_{E,o}
.

(
3
): We note that

\displaystyle\pi(t)(f_{E,o}\cdot g)=\pi(t)(f_{E,o})\cdot\pi(t)(g)\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}f_{E,o}\cdot\pi(t)(g)

Here in
(a)
we recall that
f_{E,o}
is
\mathbb{T}_{E}
-fixed.

Thus,

\displaystyle f_{E,o}\cdot g_{E,\bot}

\displaystyle=f_{E,o}\cdot g-f_{E,o}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}\pi(t)gd\varrho_{E}(t)

\displaystyle=f_{E,o}\cdot g-\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}f_{E,o}\cdot\pi(t)gd\varrho_{E}(t)

\displaystyle=f_{E,o}\cdot g-\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}\pi(t)(f_{E,o}\cdot g)d\mu_{E}(t)

\displaystyle=(f_{E,o}\cdot g)_{E,\bot}

Then we get the result.

(
4a
): For any
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\in V
with
\lVert v_{i}\rVert=1
,
1\leq i\leq n
we have

\displaystyle\big\|\mathfrak{i}(v_{1})\cdots

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}(v_{n})(f_{E,o})\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{E})}=\big\|v_{1}\cdots v_{n}(f_{E,o})\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X})}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}v_{1}\cdots v_{n}\big(\pi(t)f\big)d\varrho_{E}(t)\Big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}\pi(t)(v_{1}\cdots v_{n}f)d\varrho_{E}(t)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\|v_{1}\cdots v_{n}f\|

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{\exp(V),n}.

Here in
(a)
we use the fact that
E\subset\mathfrak{n}_{k}\subseteq Z(\mathfrak{n})
.

The above inequality implies the result.

(
5
): For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle(f_{1})_{E,o}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}\pi(t)\big(f(\mathcal{A}x)\big)d\varrho_{E}(t)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}\ f\big(\mathcal{A}([t^{-1}]\cdot x)\big)d\varrho_{E}(t)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}\ f\big([\mathcal{A}^{\prime}t^{-1}]\cdot\mathcal{A}x\big)d\varrho_{E}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E}}\ f\big([t^{-1}]\cdot\mathcal{A}x\big)d\varrho_{E}(t)

\displaystyle=f_{E,o}(\mathcal{A}x)

Here in
(a)

[t^{-1}]
denotes the equivalence; in
(b)
we note that
\mathcal{A}
preserves
\exp(E)
and
\Gamma
. Therefore,
\mathcal{A}
induces a natural automorphism on
\mathbb{T}_{E}
which we denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

(
6
): We use
p_{E}
to denote the projection from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{X}_{E}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}g(\mathcal{A}x)

\displaystyle f_{E,o}(x)d\varrho(x)\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{X}}(g_{1})_{E,o}(x)f_{E,o}(x)d\varrho(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{X}}g_{E,o}(\mathcal{A}x)f_{E,o}(x)d\varrho(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(c)}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{E}}g_{E,o}\big(\mathcal{A}_{E}\,p_{E}(x)\big)f_{E,o}(p_{E}(x))d\varrho_{1,E}(p_{E}(x)).

Here in
(a)

g_{1}(x)=g(\mathcal{A}x)
and we use (
1
); in
(b)
we use (
5
); in
(c)
we use the fact that both
g_{E,o}(\mathcal{A}x)
and
f_{E,o}
are constant along the torus bundle
\mathbb{T}_{E}
and
\mathcal{A}
preserves the torus bundle.

This implies the result.

(
7
): Let
\mathcal{A}=I_{d}
,
E=\mathfrak{n}
and
f=1
. Then the result follows from (
6
).

(
8
): We note that

\displaystyle v^{n}(f(\alpha(z))=\lVert d\alpha(z)(v)\rVert^{n}(w^{n}f)(\alpha(z))

where

w=\frac{d\alpha(z)(v)}{\lVert d\alpha(z)(v)\rVert}
; and

\displaystyle\lVert d\alpha(z)(v)\rVert\leq Ce^{\chi(z)}(1+\lVert z\rVert)^{l\dim E}\lVert v\rVert.

Appendix F
Proof of Lemma
9.1

We assume notations in (
2
) of Section
9.1
. Denote by
M^{\tau}
the transpose of
M
. The characteristic polynomial of
M^{\tau}
is also
\mathfrak{p}
. For
M^{\tau}
, similar to (
9.2
) and (
9.3
) we have the corresponding splitting

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{m}=\oplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{F}_{i}

by noting that each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is also
M^{\tau}
-invariant.

On each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, we have Lyapunov exponents

\chi_{i,1}<\cdots<\chi_{i,L(i)}
and corresponding splitting

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{i}=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,L(i)}.

Similar to (
3
), we set

\displaystyle E_{+}(M^{\tau})=\oplus_{\chi_{i,j}>0}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j},\quad E_{0}(M^{\tau})=\oplus_{\chi_{i,j}=0}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j},

\displaystyle E_{-}(M^{\tau})=\oplus_{\chi_{i,j}<0}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j}.

(
1
): Denote by
W
the following subspaces:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\max,M},\quad\mathcal{L}_{\min,M},\quad E_{+}(M),\quad E_{-}(M).

Let

W_{1}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\oplus_{j\neq L(i)}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j},&\text{if }W=\mathcal{L}_{\max,M},\\[2.84526pt]
\oplus_{j\neq 1}\mathcal{L}_{i,j},&\text{if }W=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\min,M},\\[2.84526pt]
E_{0}(M^{\tau})\oplus E_{s}(M^{\tau}),&\text{if }W=E_{u}(M),\\[2.84526pt]
E_{0}(M^{\tau})\oplus E_{u}(M^{\tau}),&\text{if }W=E_{s}(M).\end{cases}

Next, we show that

(F.1)

\displaystyle W_{1}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{m}=\{0\}.

Otherwise, suppose
v
is a non-zero integer vector inside
W_{1}
. Since
W_{1}
is
M^{\tau}
invariant,
v
generates a rational
M^{\tau}
invariant subspace
R_{v}
inside
W_{1}
. Let
p_{v}
be the characteristic polynomial of
M^{\tau}
restricted on
R_{v}
. Since
p_{v}
divides
\mathfrak{p}
,

p_{v}=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathfrak{q}_{i}^{d_{i}}
,
0\leq d_{i}\leq c_{i}
.

The case of
W_{1}=\oplus_{j\neq L(i)}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j}
or
W_{1}=\oplus_{j\neq 1}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,j}
:

Choose
1\leq i\leq l
such that
d_{i}>0
. On one hand, the construction of
W_{1}
shows that either
\chi_{i,1}
or
\chi_{i,L(i)}
is not a Lyapunov exponent of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
. On the other hand, irreducibility of

\mathfrak{q}_{i}
implies that the Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
must match the Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}
on
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
. Consequently, both
\chi_{i,1}
and
\chi_{i,L(i)}
are Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
. Thus, we have a contradiction, which implies the result.

The case of
W_{1}=E_{0}(M^{\tau})\oplus E_{-}(M^{\tau})
or
W_{1}=E_{0}(M^{\tau})\oplus E_{+}(M^{\tau})
:

On one hand, Since
M^{\tau}
has no roots o unity, neither does
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
. On the other hand, either all the Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
are
\leq 0
or all the Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
are
\geq 0
. Since
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
is represented as an integer matrix with determinant
1
or
-1
, all the Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
are
0
. It follows from a result of Kronecker
[
19
]
which
states that an integer matrix with all eigenvalues on the unit circle has to have
all eigenvalues roots of unity. Thus, we have a contradiction, which implies the result.

Next, we show that

(F.2)

\displaystyle d(z,W_{1})\geq C\lVert z\rVert^{-m},\qquad\forall\,\,0\neq z\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}.

In fact, it follows from (
F.1
) and from the following the arithmetic
properties of ergodic toral automorphisms (see e.g.
[
5
, Lemma 4.1]
for a proof):

Lemma F.1

(Katznelson’s Lemma)
.

Let
B
be an
n\times n
matrix with integer coefficients. Assume that
\mathbb{R}^{n}
splits as
\mathbb{R}^{n}=V_{1}\bigoplus V_{2}
with
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
invariant under
B

and such that
B|_{V_{1}}
and
B|_{V_{2}}
have no common eigenvalues. If
V_{1}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}=\{0\}
, then
there exists a constant
K
such that

\displaystyle d(m,V_{1})\geq K\lVert n\rVert^{-n}\quad\text{for all }0\neq m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},

where
\lVert v\rVert
denotes
Euclidean norm and
d
is Euclidean distance.

Next, we show that

\displaystyle W^{\bot}=W_{1}.

We consider the polynomial
\mathfrak{y}
defined by:

•

W=\mathcal{L}_{\max,M}
:
\mathfrak{y}=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\prod_{\sigma\neq e^{\chi_{i,L(i)}}}(x-\sigma)^{c_{i}}
. Here for each
i
, the product is over all eigenvalues
\sigma
of
M|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
with
\sigma\neq e^{\chi_{i,L(i)}}
.

•

W=\mathcal{L}_{\min,M}
:
\mathfrak{y}=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\prod_{\sigma\neq e^{\chi_{i,1}}}(x-\sigma)^{c_{i}}
. Here for each
i
, the product is over all eigenvalues
\sigma
of
M|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
with
\sigma\neq e^{\chi_{i,1}}
.

•

W=E_{+}(M)
:
\mathfrak{y}=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\prod_{|\sigma|\leq 1}(x-\sigma)^{c_{i}}
. Here for each
i
, the product is over all eigenvalues
\sigma
of
M|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
with
|\sigma|\leq 1
.

•

W=E_{-}(M)
:
\mathfrak{y}=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\prod_{|\sigma|\geq 1}(x-\sigma)^{c_{i}}
. Here for each
i
, the product is over all eigenvalues
\sigma
of
M|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
with
|\sigma|\geq 1
.

It is clear that
\mathfrak{y}
is real and thus we have

\displaystyle W^{\bot}=(\ker\mathfrak{y}(M))^{\bot}=\text{range}\,\mathfrak{y}(M^{\tau})=W_{1}.

This completes the proof.

Finally, we show that
W
is a Diophantine subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We note that

(F.3)

\displaystyle d(z,\,W^{\bot})=d(z,\,W_{1})\overset{\text{(1)}}{\geq}C\lVert z\rVert^{-m},\qquad\forall\,\,0\neq z\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
F.2
).

We use
p_{W}
to denote the projection from
\mathbb{R}^{m}
to
W
. Choose a basis
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim W}\}
of
W
. For any
0\neq z\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\dim W}|z\cdot v_{i}|

\displaystyle\geq C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim W}}\lVert p_{W}(z)\rVert

\displaystyle=C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{W}}d(z,\,W^{\bot})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\geq}C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{W}}\lVert z\rVert^{-m}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
F.3
).

Hence, we finish the proof.

(
2
): For any
1\leq i\leq l
and
1\leq j\leq L(i)
set

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{i,j}=\oplus_{k\neq j}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{i,k}.

Next, we show that

(F.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{i,j}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{\dim\mathcal{F}_{i}}=\{0\}.

Otherwise, suppose
v
is a non-zero integer vector inside
\tilde{F}_{i,j}
. Since
\tilde{F}_{i,j}
is
M^{\tau}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
invariant,
v
generates a rational
M^{\tau}
invariant subspace
R_{v}
inside
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. Let
p_{v}
be the characteristic polynomial of
M^{\tau}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
restricted on
R_{v}
.
On one hand,
\chi_{i,j}
is not a Lyapunov exponent of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
. On the hand, we note that the characteristic polynomial of
M^{\tau}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
is
\mathfrak{q}_{i}^{c_{i}}
. Since
p_{v}
divides
\mathfrak{q}_{i}^{c_{i}}
,

p_{v}=\mathfrak{q}_{i}^{d}
,
0<d\leq c_{i}
.
This implies that the Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
must match the Lyapunov exponents of
M^{\tau}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
. Consequently,
\chi_{i,j}
is a Lyapunov exponent of
M^{\tau}|_{R_{v}}
. Thus, we have a contradiction, which implies the result.

Next, we show that

\displaystyle\text{The complment of }(\mathcal{L}_{i,j})^{\bot}\text{ in }\mathcal{F}_{i}\text{ is }\mathcal{F}_{i,j}.

We consider the polynomial

\displaystyle\mathfrak{y}=\prod_{|\sigma|\neq e^{\chi_{i,j}}}(x-\sigma)^{c_{i}}.

Here the product is over all eigenvalues
\sigma
of
M^{\tau}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
with
|\sigma|\neq e^{\chi_{i,j}}
. It is clear that
\mathfrak{y}
is real and thus we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{i,j})^{\bot}=(\ker\mathfrak{y}(M|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}))^{\bot}=\text{range}\,\mathfrak{y}(M^{\tau}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}})=\tilde{F}_{i,j}.

This completes the proof.

Next, we show that

(F.5)

\displaystyle d(z,\,\tilde{F}_{i,j})\geq C\lVert z\rVert^{-\dim\mathcal{F}_{i}},\qquad\forall\,\,0\neq z\in\mathbb{Z}^{\dim\mathcal{F}_{i}}.

In fact, it follows from (
F.4
) and Lemma
F.1
.

Finally, we show that
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}
is a Diophantine subspace of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. We note that

(F.6)

\displaystyle d(z,\,(\mathcal{L}_{i,j})^{\bot})=d(z,\,\tilde{F}_{i,j})\overset{\text{(1)}}{\geq}C\lVert z\rVert^{-\dim\mathcal{F}_{i}},\qquad\forall\,\,0\neq z\in\mathbb{Z}^{\dim\mathcal{F}_{i}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
F.5
).

We use
p_{\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}
to denote the projection from
\mathcal{F}_{i}
to
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}
. Choose a basis
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}\}
of
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}
. For any
0\neq z\in\mathbb{Z}^{\dim\mathcal{F}_{i}}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}|z\cdot v_{i}|

\displaystyle\geq C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}}\lVert p_{\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}(z)\rVert

\displaystyle=C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}}d(z,\,(\mathcal{L}_{i,j})^{\bot})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\geq}C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim\mathcal{L}_{i,j}}}\lVert z\rVert^{-\dim\mathcal{F}_{i}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
F.6
).

Hence, we finish the proof.

Appendix G
Proof of Lemma
9.2

(
1
): Set
\bar{N}=N/[N,N]
and
\bar{\Gamma}=\Gamma/[\Gamma,\Gamma]
. Set
\mathbb{T}=\bar{N}/\bar{\Gamma}
. The automorphism of
\mathcal{A}
descends to an automorphism

\mathcal{A}_{1}
on
\mathbb{T}
from
\mathcal{A}
, and on the Lie algebra we have
d\mathcal{A}_{1}=d\mathcal{A}|_{\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]}
.

Since
\bar{N}
is abelian, connected and simply connected, it is isomorphic
to
\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
(see Section
8.4
), and
\bar{\Gamma}
is then a lattice in
\bar{N}
. Hence
\mathbb{T}
is a torus of dimension
n_{1}
.
There is a natural projection

\displaystyle p_{1}:N/\Gamma\to\bar{N}/\bar{\Gamma}=\mathbb{T}

whose fibers are the compact quotient
[N,N]/[\Gamma,\Gamma]
. This shows that
\mathcal{X}=N/\Gamma
is a fiber bundle over
\mathbb{T}
. Hence,
the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},dx)
, where
dx
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}
, can be naturally imbedded into
L^{2}(\mathcal{X},d\varrho)
by
viewed as functions that are trivial on the bundle
[N,N]/[\Gamma,\Gamma]
. We denote this embedding by
\iota
. Since
[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
is preserved by
d\mathcal{A}
, one checks easily that:

\displaystyle\iota(\xi\circ\mathcal{A}_{1})=\iota(\xi)\circ\mathcal{A},\qquad\forall\,\xi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},dx)

Hence ergodicity of
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{X}
implies that ergodicity of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
on
\mathbb{T}
, which completes the argument.

(
2
): Since the restriction
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}|_{V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}}
is an isomorphism and
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}
induces the rational structure on
\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
, a subspace
W\subseteq V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}
is
\Gamma
-rational if and and only if
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}(W)
is rational in
\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
. Hence, the subspaces
\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\prime}
,
1\leq i\leq l_{0}
are
\Gamma
-rational.

It follows from (
2
) of Lemma
9.1
that each
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}
is a Diophantine subspaces of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. Thus,
each
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}^{\prime}
is a Diophantine subspaces of
\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}(V_{i,j})=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{i,j}^{\prime})
,
V_{i,j}
is a Diophantine subspace for
\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\prime}\subset V_{\mathcal{E}^{1}}
of type
\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]
.

Appendix H
Proof of Lemma
9.3

We note that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}/[\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}]=\oplus_{i=1}^{l_{0}}\mathfrak{p}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\prime}).

Then we have

(H.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{k}=E_{1}+E_{2}+\cdots+E_{l_{0}}=E_{[l_{0}]}.

We note that

\displaystyle E_{[i]}+E_{i+1}=E_{[i+1]},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}-1.

This gives a direct sum decomposition

(H.2)

\displaystyle E_{[i]}\oplus E_{i}^{\prime}=E_{[i+1]},\qquad 1\leq i\leq l_{0}-1,

where

(H.3)

\displaystyle E_{i+1}=(E_{[i]}\cap E_{i+1})\oplus E_{i}^{\prime},

and
E_{i}^{\prime}
is also a
\Gamma
-rational subspace.

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
, we can write

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{l_{0}-1}(f_{E_{[i]},o}-f_{E_{[i+1]},o})+f_{E_{[l_{0}]},o})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\sum_{i=0}^{l_{0}-1}(f_{E_{[i]},o}-f_{E_{[i+1]},o})+f_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},o}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}\sum_{i=0}^{l_{0}-1}(f_{E_{[i]},o})_{E_{i+1},\bot}+f_{\mathfrak{n}_{k},o}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
H.1
); in
(2)
recalling (
1
) of Section
7.1
, we have

\displaystyle f_{E_{[i]},o}

\displaystyle=(f_{E_{[i]},o})_{E_{i+1},o}+(f_{E_{[i]},o})_{E_{i+1},\bot}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E_{i+1}}}\pi(t)(f_{E_{[i]},o})d\varrho_{E_{i+1}}(t)+(f_{E_{[i]},o})_{E_{i+1},\bot}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E_{i}^{\prime}}}\pi(t)(f_{E_{[i]},o})d\varrho_{E_{i}^{\prime}}(t)+(f_{E_{[i]},o})_{E_{i+1},\bot}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E_{i}^{\prime}}}\pi(t)\Big(\int_{\mathbb{T}_{E_{[i]}}}\pi(s)fd\varrho_{E_{[i]}}(s)\Big)d\varrho_{E_{i+1}}(t)+(f_{E_{[i]},o})_{E_{i+1},\bot}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}f_{E_{[i+1]},o}+(f_{E_{[i]},o})_{E_{i+1},\bot}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
H.3
); in
(2)
we use (
H.2
).

Appendix I
Proof of Lemma
9.10

Firstly, we show that: there is
z_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{0},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}.

Choose an arbitrary
z_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}\backslash\{0\}
. If
\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}
, then we are done.
Otherwise, we have

\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}}\mathfrak{n}^{(z,2)}\subsetneq\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)},

so there exists
z_{2}^{\prime}\neq 0
with

(I.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2}^{\prime},2)}\subsetneq\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}.

Next we claim that there is
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime}+z_{1},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime},2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}.

It is clear that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime}+z_{1},2)}\supseteq\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime},2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2}^{\prime},2)}\quad\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N}.

On the other hand, we consider the generalized eigenspace decomposition for
d\alpha
restricted on the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
subgroup generated by
z_{2}^{\prime}
and
z_{1}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}=\oplus_{\lambda\in\Theta}\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}

such that for any
\lambda\in\Theta
, the eigenvalues of
d\alpha(mz_{2}^{\prime}+z_{1})|_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}
is
\lambda(z_{2})^{m}\lambda(z_{1})
.

It is easy to check that for any
\lambda\in\Theta
, if either
\lambda(z_{2})
or
\lambda(z_{1})
is not a root of unity, then there is only finitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lambda(z_{2})^{m}\lambda(z_{1})
is a root of unity. By choosing
m
outside the union of these finitely many “bad” integers (over all
\lambda
), we guarantee that
d\alpha(mz_{2}^{\prime}+z_{1})

acquires
no new
root of unity eigenvalues beyond those already in

\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2}^{\prime},2)}
. Hence

(I.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime}+z_{1},2)}\subseteq\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2}^{\prime},2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime},2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}.

Thus, we proved the claim.

Let
z_{2}=mz_{2}^{\prime}+z_{1}
. Then from (
I.1
) and (
I.2
) we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(mz_{2}^{\prime}+z_{1},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2}^{\prime},2)}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}\subsetneq\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{1},2)}.

If
\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}
, then we are done. Otherwise, we repeat the above process to find
z_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}\backslash\{0\}
, such that
\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{3},2)}\subsetneq\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{2},2)}
. This implies that we can continue this process until we find
z_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}\backslash\{0\}
such that
\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{k},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}
. Then we finished the proof.

Finally, we show that: there is a regular
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
such that
\mathfrak{n}^{(z,2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}
. Regularity means avoiding the finitely many Lyapunov hyperplanes

\{\chi=0\}
and coincidence hyperplanes
\{\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}\}
. Hence there
are infinitely many regular vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{l}
; pick one
w
.

Let
z_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
such that
\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{0},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}
.
Consider
z(n)=n\,z_{0}+w
. Each forbidden condition (lying in a hyperplane, or lying in a coincidence hyperplane,
or introducing a new root of unity eigenvalue on the complement of

\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{0},2)}
) excludes at most finitely many integers
n
. Thus
for some
n
,
z=z(n)
is regular and still satisfies

\mathfrak{n}^{(z,2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(z_{0},2)}=\mathfrak{n}^{(2)}
.
